in the supplement). This result is a type of Brascamp-Lieb inequality
[
16
,
60
]
. There is an existing literature on Brascamp-Lieb inequality in relation to information theoretical problems, in relation to mutual information
[
28
,
61
,
62
]
, in addition to the communication constraint testing problem in
[
77
]
. That (
41
) has a “Gaussian maximizer” allows tractable analysis of the chi-square divergence in (
40
), yielding that the latter display is further lower bounded by

1-\sqrt{(1/2)\int\left(\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}-1\right)d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u)}+\pi(H_{\rho}^{c}),

(42)

where

\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}:=\int e^{f^{\top}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Xi^{j}_{u}g}d(\pi\times\pi)(f,g),\quad\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}:=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{{Y^{(j)}}|U=u}\left(\frac{d{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}}{d{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}}\right),

(43)

where
\Xi^{j}_{u}
denotes the covariance of (a subset of) the data
X^{(j)}_{L}
(defined as in (
44
)) conditionally on the transcript
Y^{(j)}
and
U=u
;

\Xi_{u}^{j}:={\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\sigma^{-1}{X}^{(j)}_{L;i}\bigg|Y^{(j)},U=u\right]{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\sigma^{-1}{X}^{(j)}_{L;i}\bigg|Y^{(j)},U=u\right]^{\top}.

(44)

Whilst the quantities
\mathrm{A}_{u}
and
\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}
are still not fully tractable, sharp bounds for both are possible and form the content of Steps 4 and 5, respectively.

4.

As remarked earlier, class of local randomness protocols is a strictly smaller class. To attain the sharper (i.e. larger) lower bound for local randomness protocols, we exploit the fact that Step 2 allows us to choose the prior adversarially to the distribution of the transcripts. In particular, since
U
is degenerate in the case of local randomness only, this means that the covariance of
\pi
to be more diffuse in the directions in which
\Xi^{j}_{u}
is the smallest. When considering shared randomness protocols,
U
is not degenerate, and the lower bound follows by taking the covariance of
\pi
to be an order
2^{L}
-rank approximation of the identity map on
\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})
. The bounds for
\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}
are

\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}=\exp\left({C\frac{\rho^{4}}{c_{\alpha}2^{3L}}\text{Tr}\left(\Xi_{u}\right)^{2}}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}\leq\exp\left({C\frac{\rho^{4}}{c_{\alpha}2^{2L}}\|\Xi_{u}\|\text{Tr}\left(\Xi_{u}\right)}\right)

(45)

for local randomness protocols and shared randomness protocols, respectively.

5.

So far, Steps 1-4 have not used the fact that the transcripts are necessarily less informative than the original data, as a consequence of the transcripts being
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP. In this step, we exploit the privacy constraint to argue that
\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}
and
\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}
are small at the detection boundary for
\rho
.

In order to capture the information loss due to privacy in
\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}
, it suffices to bound the trace and operator norm of
\Xi_{u}
. The quantity
\Xi_{u}
can be seen as the Fisher information of the finite dimensional submodel spanned by the covariance of
\pi
. This quantity, loosely speaking, captures how much information the transcript contains on the original data. In order to analyze
\Xi_{u}
, we rely on a “score attack” type of technique, as employed in
[
23
,
20
]
.

The quantity
\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}
corresponds to the (product of) the local likelihoods of the transcripts. Whenever
\epsilon\geq 1/\sqrt{n}
, it suffices to consider the trivial bound

{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{{Y^{(j)}}|U=u}\left(\frac{d{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}}{d{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}}\right)\leq{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{{X^{(j)}}|U=u}\left(\frac{d{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{X^{(j)}}}{d{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{X^{(j)}}}\right),

(46)

and further bounding the right-hand side without privacy specific arguments. Whenever
\epsilon<1/\sqrt{n}
, more sophisticated methods are need to capture the effect of privacy. Our argument uses a coupling method, which, combined with the fact that the likelihoods of the transcripts are bounded in our construction, allows us to obtain a sharp bound for
\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}
. After obtaining the bounds in terms of the rank
2^{L}
and
\rho
, the proof is finished by choosing
L
such that the second and third term in (
40
) are balanced (minimizing their sum).

6
Discussion

The findings in this paper highlight the trade-off between statistical accuracy and privacy in federated goodness-of-fit testing under differential privacy (DP) constraints. We characterize the problem in terms of the minimax separation rate, which quantifies the difficulty of the testing problem based on the regularity of the underlying function, the sample size, the degree of data distribution, and the stringency of the DP constraint. The minimax separation rate varies depending on whether the distributed testing protocol has access to local or shared randomness. Furthermore, we construct data-driven adaptive testing procedures that achieve the same optimal performance, up to logarithmic factors, even when the regularity of the functional parameter is unknown.

One possible extension of this work is to consider a more general distribution of the privacy budget across the servers. Our current analysis supports differing budgets to the extent that
\epsilon_{j}\asymp\epsilon_{k}
,
\delta_{j}\asymp\delta_{k}
, and
n_{j}\asymp n_{k}
. However, one could explore more heterogeneous settings where severs differ significantly in their differential privacy constraints and number of observations. Although this would complicate the presentation of results, the techniques developed in this paper could, in principle, be extended to such settings.

Another interesting direction is to consider multiple testing problems, where the goal is to test multiple hypotheses simultaneously. We anticipate that the framework, insights, and theoretical results provided in the current paper will serve as valuable resources for future studies in this domain.

Regarding adaptation, not much is known about the cost of privacy outside the local DP setting (i.e., one observation per server;
n=1
in our context). Interestingly, the cost of adaptation is minimal in the privacy setting considered in this paper. It remains an open question whether this minimal cost is a general phenomenon, whether it can be characterized exactly, or whether the cost of adaptation is more severe in other settings. We leave these questions for future research.

A
Proof of the lower bound Theorem
5

In this section, we provide a proof for Theorem
5
. The proof is divided into several steps, following the outline provided in Section
5
.

It is convenient to introduce the following notation. In the notation that follows, we shall consider a local randomness protocol to consist of a triplet

(T,\{\left({\mathbb{P}}^{Y^{(j)}|X^{(j)}=x,U=u}\right)_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}^{n},u\in{\mathcal{U}}}\}_{j=1}^{m},({\mathcal{U}},\mathscr{U},{\mathbb{P}}^{U})),

where the probability space
({\mathcal{U}},\mathscr{U},{\mathbb{P}}^{U})
has the trivial sigma-algebra;
\mathscr{U}=\{\emptyset,{\mathcal{U}}\}
. This allows streamlining the argument for both lower bounds of Theorem
5
into a single argument. Given an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP distributed protocol triplet

(T,\{\left({\mathbb{P}}^{Y^{(j)}|X^{(j)}=x,U=u}\right)_{x\in{\mathcal{X}}^{n},u\in{\mathcal{U}}}\}_{j=1}^{m},({\mathcal{U}},\mathscr{U},{\mathbb{P}}^{U})),

we shall use the notation
P_{f}={\mathbb{P}}_{f}^{X^{(j)}}
. For the Markov kernel

(A,(x,u))\mapsto{\mathbb{P}}^{Y^{(j)}|(X^{(j)},U)=(x,u)}(A),

we shall use the shorthand
(A,(x,u))\mapsto K^{j}(A|(x,u))
. If the transcript
Y^{(j)}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP, the Markov kernel satisfies

\displaystyle K^{j}(A

\displaystyle|x_{1},\dots,x_{i},\dots,x_{n},u)\leq e^{\epsilon}K^{j}(A|x_{1},\dots,x_{i}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n},u)+\delta

\displaystyle\text{ for all }A\in\mathscr{Y},\;x_{i}^{\prime},x_{1},\dots,x_{i},\dots,x_{n}\in{\mathcal{X}},\;i\in\{1,\dots,n\}.

When the parameter underlying the true distribution of the data
F
is drawn from a prior distribution on
\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})
, we obtain that the distributed testing protocol satisfies the Markov chain

{\quad F}\qquad\begin{matrix}\put(-10.0,-4.0){\vector{3}{1}{20.0}}&\quad(X^{(1)},U)&\put(0.0,5.0){\vector{1}{0}{20.0}}&\qquad Y^{(1)}\quad&\put(-10.0,4.0){\vector{3}{-1}{20.0}}\\
\put(-10.0,3.0){\vector{1}{0}{20.0}}&\quad\vdots&\put(0.0,4.0){\vector{1}{0}{20.0}}&\qquad\vdots\quad&\put(-10.0,4.0){\vector{1}{0}{20.0}}\\
\put(-10.0,12.0){\vector{3}{-1}{20.0}}&\quad(X^{(m)},U)&\put(0.0,5.0){\vector{1}{0}{20.0}}&\qquad Y^{(m)}\quad&\put(-10.0,5.0){\vector{3}{1}{20.0}}\end{matrix}\qquad T,

(S.1)

with
{\mathbb{P}}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}_{f}=P_{f}K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)
for all
j\in[m]
. Let
K_{u}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}K^{j}(\cdot|\cdot,u)
denote the product conditional distribution with
U=u
, such that distribution of the collection of transcripts conditionally on
U=u
then satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|U=u}_{f}=P^{m}_{f}K_{u}()
.

If the transcript
Y^{(j)}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP and generated from
K^{j}_{u},u\in{\mathcal{U}}
, this translates to

\displaystyle K^{j}(A

\displaystyle|x_{1},\dots,x_{i},\dots,x_{n},u)\leq e^{\epsilon}K^{j}(A|x_{1},\dots,x_{i}^{\prime},\dots,x_{n},u)+\delta

(S.2)

\displaystyle\text{ for all }A\in\mathscr{Y},\;x_{i}^{\prime},x_{1},\dots,x_{i},\dots,x_{n}\in{\mathcal{X}},\;i\in\{1,\dots,n\}.

A.1
Step 1: Lower bounding the testing risk by the Bayes risk

As a first step, we lower bound the testing risk by the Bayes risk. Following the notation introduced above, let
T\equiv(T,\{{K}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m},({\mathcal{U}},\mathscr{U},{\mathbb{P}}^{U}))
be an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP distributed testing protocol and let
{K}_{u}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}{K}^{j}(\cdot|\cdot,u)
. The testing risk for
T
can be written as

{\mathcal{R}}(H_{\rho}^{{s},R},T)=\int P_{0}^{m}K_{u}Td{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u)+\underset{f\in H_{\rho}^{s,R}}{\sup}\int P_{f}^{m}K_{u}(1-T)d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u).

(S.3)

Consider
L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
d_{L}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}2^{l}
and consider
\pi=N(0,c_{\alpha}^{-1/2}d^{-1}_{L}\rho^{2}\bar{\Gamma})
for a symmetric idempotent matrix
\bar{\Gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}\times d_{L}}
. Consider also the linear operator
\Psi_{L}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}}\to\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})
defined by
\Psi_{L}\tilde{f}=f
for

f_{lk}=\begin{cases}\tilde{f}_{lk}&\text{if }l\leq L,\\
0&\text{if }l>L,\end{cases}

(S.4)

for
\tilde{f}=(\tilde{f}_{11},\dots,\tilde{f}_{L2^{L}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}}
. Since
\Psi_{L}
is measurable, any probability distribution
\pi_{L}
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2^{L}}
,
\pi_{L}\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}
defines a probability measure on the Borel sigma algebra of
\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})
.

Using that
0\leq T\leq 1
, the above testing risk is lower bounded by the Bayes risk

\int\left(P_{0}^{m}{K}_{u}T+\int P_{f}^{m}{K}_{u}(1-T)d\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}(f)\right)d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u)-\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}\left((H_{\rho}^{s,R})^{c}\right).

(S.5)

We highlight here that
\bar{\Gamma}
can depend on
\{{K}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
.

To lower bound the testing risk further, it suffices to show that the prior
\pi
has little mass outside of
H_{\rho}^{s,R}
. This follows by a standard Gaussian concentration argument, provided in Lemma
5
below, from which it follows that, for
c_{\alpha}>0
small enough, it holds that
\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}((H_{\rho}^{s,R})^{c})\leq\alpha/4
. This yields that (
S.5
) is lower bounded by

\int\left(P_{0}^{m}{K}_{u}T+\int P_{f}^{m}{K}_{u}(1-T)d\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}(f)\right)d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u)-\alpha/4.

(S.6)

Lemma 5
.

Suppose
\rho\lesssim c_{\alpha}2^{-Ls}
. Then, for any
c_{\alpha}>0
small enough, it holds that
\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}((H_{\rho}^{s,R}))\leq\alpha/4
.

Let
f\in\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})
. It holds that
f\in H_{\rho}^{s,R}
if and only if
\|f\|_{2}^{2}\geq\rho^{2}
and
\|f\|_{{\mathcal{B}}^{s}_{p,q}}\leq R
. For the first of these events, we have that

\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}(f:\|f\|_{2}^{2}\geq\rho^{2})=\text{Pr}\left(Z^{\top}\bar{\Gamma}Z\geq\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}d_{L}\right),

(S.7)

where
Z\sim N(0,I_{d_{L}})
. Using that
\bar{\Gamma}
is idempotent, the right-hand side of the above display can be made arbitrarily small for small enough choice of
c_{\alpha}>0
, by Lemma
36
.

To assure that
f\sim\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}
concentrates on a Besov ball, we recall the definition of the Besov norm as given in (
5
). We have that

\displaystyle\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}(f:\|f\|_{{\mathcal{B}}^{s}_{p,q}}\leq R)

\displaystyle=\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}\left(2^{L({s}+1/2-1/p)}\left\|(f_{Lk})_{k=1}^{2^{l}}\right\|_{p}\leq R\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Pr}\left(2^{-L/p}\left\|Z\right\|_{p}\leq CR/c_{\alpha}^{3/4}\right)

where
Z\sim N(0,I_{2^{L}})
and
C>0
a constant. The right-hand side of the above display can be made arbitrarily small for small enough choice of
c_{\alpha}>0
, following from the fact that
Z
is a standard normal vector
{\mathbb{E}}\|Z\|_{p}^{p}\lesssim 2^{L}
, where the constant depends on
p
(see e.g. Proposition 2.5.2 in
[
83
]
) and Markov’s inequality.
∎

The larger
L
, the larger the effective dimension of the signal under the alternative hypothesis. Setting
L
such that
2^{L}\asymp\rho^{-1/s}
means that the requirement of the above lemma are satisfied and consequently the Gaussian prior most of it its mass in the alternative hypothesis. Recalling that
d_{L}\asymp 2^{L}
, the condition (
35
) in the case of local randomness protocols can be written as

\rho^{2}\lesssim c_{\alpha}\sigma^{2}\left(\frac{d_{L}^{3/2}}{mn(n\epsilon^{2}\wedge d_{L})}\bigwedge\left(\frac{\sqrt{d_{L}}}{\sqrt{m}n\sqrt{n\epsilon^{2}\wedge 1}}\bigvee\frac{1}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right)\right),

(S.8)

and in the case of shared randomness protocols,
(
37
) can be written as

\rho^{2}\lesssim c_{\alpha}\sigma^{2}\left(\frac{d_{L}}{mn\sqrt{n\epsilon^{2}\wedge 1}\sqrt{n\epsilon^{2}\wedge d_{L}}}\bigwedge\left(\frac{\sqrt{d_{L}}}{\sqrt{m}n\sqrt{n\epsilon^{2}\wedge 1}}\bigvee\frac{1}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right)\right).

(S.9)

A.2
Step 2: Approximating the distribution of the transcripts

In Step 3, we aim to use the Brascamp-Lieb type inequality Lemma 10.1 of
[
77
]
, which we restate as Lemma
12
below. However, the
forward-backward channel
corresponding to
{K}_{u}
,
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto{q}_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})
, defined as

{q}_{u}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\int\frac{d{K}(\cdot|x_{1},u)}{d{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}}(y)\frac{d{K}(\cdot|x_{2},u)}{d{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}}(y)d{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|U=u}_{0}(y),

(S.10)

is possibly unbounded when
\delta>0
. To overcome this, we use Lemma
6
below to construct
(\epsilon,3\delta)
-DP Markov kernels
\{\tilde{K}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
such that the corresponding forward-backward channel
\tilde{q}_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})
is bounded. The construction of
\{\tilde{K}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
is such that the total variation distance between
\tilde{K}^{j}
and
\tilde{K}^{j}
is small.

Lemma 6
.

For any
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP collection of Markov kernels
\{K^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
, there exists a collection of
(\epsilon,3\delta)
-DP kernels
\{\tilde{K}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
such that for some fixed constant
C>0
,

\underset{x\in{\mathcal{X}}^{n}}{\sup}\;\frac{d\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|x)}{dP_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})}(y)<C,\;P_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})\text{-almost surely},

(S.11)

whilst

\|P_{f}(K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})-\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot,X^{(j)}))\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2m}.

For any
x\in{\mathcal{X}}^{n}
and set
A\in\mathcal{\mathscr{Y}}^{(j)}
, we have that

K^{j}(A|x)=\int_{A}\frac{dK^{j}(\cdot|x)}{dP_{0}K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})}(y)dP_{0}K^{j}(y|X^{(j)})\leq 1,

where we note that by Lemma
39
, the density of the integrand exists. So, by Markov’s inequality, there exists a set
A_{x}^{M}\in\mathcal{\mathscr{Y}}^{(j)}
such that

\frac{dK^{j}(\cdot|x)}{dP_{0}K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})}(y)\leq M\;\text{ on }A_{x}^{M},

whilst

K^{j}\left((A_{x}^{M})^{c}|x\right)\leq 1/M.

(S.12)

Define for all
x\in{\mathcal{X}}
,

\tilde{K}^{j}(B|x):=\frac{K^{j}(B\cap A_{x}^{M}|x)}{K^{j}(A_{x}^{M}|x)},

which can be rewritten as

K^{j}\left(B\cap A_{x}^{M}|x\right)+K^{j}\left((A_{x}^{M})^{c}|x\right)\frac{K^{j}(B\cap A_{x}^{M}|x)}{K^{j}(A_{x}^{M}|x)}.

(S.13)

Then,
\tilde{K}^{j}
is
(\epsilon,3\delta)
-DP whenever
M>4\delta^{-1}
; for any
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{n}
that are Hamming distance
1
-apart and
B\in\mathscr{Y}^{(j)}
,

\displaystyle\tilde{K}^{j}(B|x)

\displaystyle\leq K^{j}\left(B|x\right)+K^{j}\left((A_{x}^{M})^{c}|x\right)\frac{K^{j}\left(B\cap A_{x}^{M}|x\right)}{K^{j}\left(A_{x}^{M}|x\right)}

\displaystyle=K^{j}\left(B\cap A_{x^{\prime}}^{M}|x\right)+K^{j}\left(B\cap(A_{x^{\prime}}^{M})^{c}|x\right)+K^{j}\left((A_{x}^{M})^{c}|x\right)\frac{K^{j}\left(B\cap A_{x}^{M}|x\right)}{K^{j}\left(A_{x}^{M}|x\right)}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\epsilon}K^{j}\left(B\cap A_{x^{\prime}}^{M}|x^{\prime}\right)+e^{\epsilon}K^{j}\left(B\cap(A_{x^{\prime}}^{M})^{c}|x^{\prime}\right)+2\delta+\frac{1}{M}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\epsilon}\tilde{K}^{j}\left(B|x^{\prime}\right)+(1+e^{\epsilon})M^{-1}+2\delta,

where the second to last inequality follows by (
S.12
) and the last inequality follows by simply adding the nonnegative second term in (
S.13
). Furthermore, its Radon-Nikodym derivative satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{d\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|x)}{dP_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})}(y)

\displaystyle\leq 2\text{1}_{A_{x}^{M}}\frac{dK^{j}(\cdot|x)}{dP_{0}{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})}(y)\leq 2M

P_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})
-almost surely. Moreover, it holds for any
f\in\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})
that

\displaystyle\|P_{f}^{n}(K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})-\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot,X^{(j)}))\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq\int\|K^{j}(\cdot|x)-\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|x)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}dP_{f}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 2\int|K^{j}\left((A_{x}^{M})^{c}|x\right)|dP_{f}^{n}(x)\leq\frac{2}{M}.

Since a choice
M>\delta^{-1}\vee 2m/\alpha
yields the bound uniformly in
x\in{\mathcal{X}}^{n}
, the result follows.
∎

By standard arguments (see also Lemma
9
), the first term in (
S.6
) can be lower bounded as follows;

\displaystyle P_{0}^{m}{K}_{u}T

\displaystyle=P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}T+P_{0}^{m}{K}_{u}T-P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}T

\displaystyle\geq P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}T-\sum_{j=1}^{m}\|P_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})-P_{0}{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

The same argument on the second term yields that, we obtain that (
S.6
) is lower bounded by

\int\left(P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}T+\int P_{f}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}(1-T)d\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}(f)\right)d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u)-3\alpha/8,

(S.14)

for an
(\epsilon,3\delta)
-DP distributed testing protocol
\tilde{T}=(T,\{\tilde{K}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m},({\mathcal{U}},\mathscr{U},{\mathbb{P}}^{U}))
.

Another issue suffered by
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP Markov kernels with
\delta>0
, is that one has very poor control over the higher moments of the local likelihoods

{{\mathcal{L}}}^{j}_{\pi,u}(y):=\frac{dP_{\pi}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)}{dP_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)}(y),

where
P_{\pi}(A):=\int P_{f}(A)d\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}(f)
, which are required to sufficiently bound the corresponding quantity
\mathrm{B}_{u}
defined in (
S.28
) in Step 5. Using similar ideas as in the proof of Lemma
6
, we can construct approximating kernels
\{\breve{K}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
such that the likelihoods
{\mathcal{L}}^{j}_{\pi,u}
are bounded. This is the content of the following lemma.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
,
\pi=N(0,c_{\alpha}^{-1/2}d^{-1/2}_{L}\rho^{2}\bar{\Gamma})
for an arbitrary positive semidefinite
\bar{\Gamma}
and let
\{K^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
correspond to a
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP distributed protocol
T
(i.e.
K^{j}
satisfies (
S.2
)). Furthermore, assume that
\epsilon\leq 1/\sqrt{n}
and define for
j=1,\dots,m
the events

A_{j,u}:=\left\{y:|{\mathcal{L}}_{\pi,u}^{j}(y)-1|\leq\frac{4m^{1/2}}{{\alpha}}\right\}

and define

\tilde{K}^{j}(B|x,u):=K^{j}(B\cap A_{j,u}|x,u)+K^{j}(A_{j,u}^{c}|x,u)\frac{P_{0}K^{j}(B\cap A_{j,u}|X^{(j)},u)}{P_{0}K^{j}(A_{j,u}|X^{(j)},u)}.

Suppose in addition that
\delta\leq c_{\alpha}/m
. Then,

(a)

The collection
\{\tilde{K}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
are
(\epsilon,2\delta)
-DP Markov kernels.

(b)

It holds
P_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)
-a.s. that

\tilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\pi,u}^{j}(y):=\int\frac{d\tilde{K}^{j}(y|x,u)}{dP_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(y|X^{(j)},u)}dP_{\pi}^{n}(x)

satisfies

|\tilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\pi,u}^{j}(y)-1|\leq\frac{5m^{1/2}}{{\alpha}}.

(S.15)

(c)

If
\rho
satisfies (
35
) or (
37
) with
c_{\alpha}>0
small enough, it holds that

\displaystyle P_{0}^{m}KT+\int P_{f}^{m}K(1-T)d\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}(f)\geq P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}T+\int P_{f}^{m}\tilde{K}(1-T)d\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}(f)-\alpha,

where
\tilde{K}
is the product kernel corresponding to
\{\tilde{K}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
.

(d)

If
K^{j}
satisfies (
S.11
), then
\tilde{K}^{j}
satisfies the same bound for some constant
C>0
.

We prove this lemma at the end of this section. The lemma above, in combination with the same argument as before (e.g. Lemma
9
) allows us to replace the
(\epsilon,3\delta)
-DP Markov kernel
\tilde{K}^{j}
of (
S.14
) with an
(\epsilon,6\delta)
-DP Markov kernel
\breve{K}^{j}
, whose transcripts have bounded likelihoods in the sense of (
S.15
). Furthermore, by part
(c)
of the lemma, the Bayes risk of (
S.14
) is further lower bounded by

\int\left(P_{0}^{m}\breve{K}_{u}T+\int P_{f}^{m}\breve{K}_{u}(1-T)d\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}_{L}(f)\right)d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u)-\alpha/2,

(S.16)

where the
\breve{K}^{j}
’s satisfy (
S.11
).

A.2.1
Proof of Lemma
7

The first statement follows by Lemma
8
below. For the second statement, we first note that by Lemma
17
proven in Section
A.5
, it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{c_{\alpha}^{1/4}}{m^{1/2}}+\delta+\frac{c_{\alpha}}{m^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\geq(P_{\pi}-P_{0})K^{j}\left(\{|{\mathcal{L}}^{j}_{\pi,u}-1|\geq 4m^{1/2}/\alpha\}|X^{(j)},u\right)

\displaystyle=P_{0}K^{j}\left(({\mathcal{L}}^{j}_{\pi,u}-1)\text{1}\{|{\mathcal{L}}^{j}_{\pi,u}-1|\geq 4m^{1/2}/\alpha\}|X^{(j)},u\right)

\displaystyle\geq 4\frac{m^{1/2}}{\alpha}P_{0}K^{j}\left(|{\mathcal{L}}^{j}_{\pi,u}-1|\geq 4m^{1/2}/\alpha|X^{(j)},u\right),

where the second inequality follows from the fact that
m^{1/2}/\alpha\geq 1
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{j}_{\pi,u}\geq 0
. Using that
\delta\leq c_{\alpha}/m
, we obtain that

P_{0}K^{j}(A_{j,u}^{c}|X^{(j)},u)\leq\frac{{\alpha(c_{\alpha}^{1/4}+c_{\alpha}(1+m^{-1}))}}{4{m}}:=\eta_{\alpha}.

(S.17)

Since
K^{j}(B|x,u)\leq\tilde{K}^{j}(B|x,u)
for all measurable
B\subset A_{j,u}
and
P_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)
has no support outside of
A_{j,u}
, it holds that

\frac{d{K}^{j}(\cdot|x,u)}{dP_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)}(y)\leq\frac{d{K}^{j}(\cdot|x,u)}{dP_{0}{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)}(y),

for all
y\in A_{j,u}
(and hence
P_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)
-a.s.). Similarly, we have for
P_{\pi}
-a.s. all
x
’s that

\frac{K^{j}(A_{j,u}^{c}|x,u)}{P_{0}K^{j}(A_{j,u}|X^{(j)},u)}\frac{dP_{0}K^{j}(\cdot\cap A_{j,u}|X^{(j)},u)}{dP_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)}(y)\leq\frac{K^{j}(A_{j,u}^{c}|x,u)}{P_{0}K^{j}(A_{j,u}|X^{(j)},u)}\leq\frac{1}{1-\eta_{\alpha}},

using that
K^{j}\leq 1
and
P_{0}K^{j}(A_{j,u}|X^{(j)},u)\geq 1-\eta_{\alpha}
. By standard arguments and the above two statements, it follows that

\displaystyle\int\frac{d\tilde{K}^{j}(y|x,u)}{dP_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(y|X^{(j)},u)}dP_{\pi}^{n}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\text{1}_{A_{j,u}}(y)\int\frac{d{K}^{j}(y|x,u)}{dP_{0}{K}^{j}(y|X^{(j)},u)}dP_{\pi}^{n}(x)+\frac{1}{1-\eta_{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\text{1}_{A_{j,u}}(y){{\mathcal{L}}}_{\pi,u}^{j}(y)+\frac{1}{1-\eta_{\alpha}}.

Applying the definition of the event
A_{j,u}
and using that
\alpha\leq 1
, we obtain that for
c_{\alpha}>0
small enough

\displaystyle\tilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\pi,u}^{j}-1\leq\frac{4m^{1/2}}{{\alpha}}+\frac{1}{1-\eta_{\alpha}}-1\leq\frac{5m^{1/2}}{{\alpha}}.

Using that
\tilde{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\pi,u}^{j}-1\geq-1
, we obtain (
S.15
), proving statement
(b)
.

For the third statement, we will aim to apply Lemma
9
. By the construction of
\tilde{K}^{j}
and the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\|P_{0}K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)-P_{0}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\|P_{0}K^{j}(\cdot\cap A_{j,u}^{c}|X^{(j)},u)\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

The latter is bounded by
{{\alpha}}/({2{m}})
(see (
S.17
)). Similarly,

\displaystyle\|P_{\pi}K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)-P_{\pi}\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq 2P_{\pi}K^{j}(A_{j,u}^{c}|X^{(j)},u).

By Lemma
17
,

\displaystyle P_{\pi}K^{j}(A_{j,u}^{c}|X^{(j)},u)\leq\left(1+c_{\alpha}^{1/4}m^{-1/2}\right)P_{0}K^{j}(A_{j}^{c}|X^{(j)},u)+\delta+\frac{c_{\alpha}}{m^{3/2}}.

Again using (
S.17
) and the fact that
\delta\leq c_{\alpha}/m
yield that the latter is also bounded by
\alpha/4m
for
c_{\alpha}>0
small enough. The condition and small enough choice of
c_{\alpha}>0
yields that the conditions of Lemma
9
and the conclusion of (c) follows. Finally, if
K^{j}
satisfies (
S.11
), the last statement follows directly by the construction of
\tilde{K}^{j}
.
∎

We finish the section by providing the two technical lemmas mentioned in the earlier proofs above. We omit the presence of the shared randomness
U
in the statement of the lemmas, as it is of no consequence to the arguments below.

Lemma 8
.

Let
K
be a Markov kernel from
({\mathcal{X}},\mathscr{X})^{n}
to
({\mathcal{Y}},\mathscr{Y})
satisfying an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP constraint (i.e. (
S.2
)) and define for a
A\in\mathscr{Y}
and a probability measure
\mu
on
\mathscr{Y}

\tilde{K}(B|x):=K(B\cap A|x)+K(A^{c}|x)\mu(B),\text{ for }x\in{\mathcal{X}},\,B\in\mathscr{Y}.

Then,
\tilde{K}
is a Markov kernel
({\mathcal{X}},\mathscr{X})
to
({\mathcal{Y}},\mathscr{Y})
satisfying an
(\epsilon,2\delta)
-DP constraint.

First,
\tilde{K}
can be seen to be a Markov kernel, as the necessary measurability assumptions hold by construction and

\tilde{K}({\mathcal{Y}}|x)=K({\mathcal{Y}}\cap A|x)+K(A^{c}|x)=1,

where it is used that
\mu
is a probability measure. Furthermore, for arbitrary
B
and
x,x^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{X}}^{n}
such that
d_{H}(x,x^{\prime})\leq 1
, it holds that

\displaystyle\tilde{K}(B|x)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\epsilon}K(B\cap A|x^{\prime})+\delta+e^{\epsilon}K(A^{c}|x^{\prime})\mu(B)+\mu(B)\delta

\displaystyle\leq e^{\epsilon}\tilde{K}(B|x^{\prime})+2\delta.

∎

Lemma 9
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
be given. Let
(T,\{K^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m})
be a distributed testing protocol and suppose that there exist kernels
\{\tilde{K}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
such that for
j=1,\dots,m
,

\|P_{0}(K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)-\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u))\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2m}\;\;{\mathbb{P}}^{U}\text{-a.s}

and

\|P_{\pi}(K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)-\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u))\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2m},\;\;{\mathbb{P}}^{U}\text{-a.s}

Then,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}^{U}P_{0}^{m}

\displaystyle K(T(Y)|X,U)+{\mathbb{P}}^{U}\int P_{f}^{m}K(1-T(Y)|X,U))d\pi(f)\geq

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}^{U}P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}(T(Y)|X,U)+{\mathbb{P}}^{U}\int P_{f}^{m}\tilde{K}(1-T(Y)|X,U))d\pi(f)-\alpha,

for the same collection of distributions.

We omit the dependence of
u
in the proof, as it is of no consequence to the arguments below. Using standard arguments,

\displaystyle P_{0}^{m}K(T(Y)=1|X)+\int P_{f}^{m}K(T(Y)=0|X)d\pi(f)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}(T(Y)=1|X)+\int P_{f}^{m}\tilde{K}(T(Y)=0|X)d\pi(f)-

\displaystyle\|P_{0}^{m}(K(\cdot|X)-\tilde{K}(\cdot|X))\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle-\|P_{\pi}^{m}(K(\cdot|X)-\tilde{K}(\cdot|X))\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

Standard arguments (see e.g. Lemma
40
in this supplement),

\|P_{\pi}^{m}(K(\cdot|X)-\tilde{K}(\cdot|X))\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}\|P_{\pi}(K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)})-\tilde{K}^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)}))\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

By applying the same lemma to
\|P_{0}^{m}(K(\cdot|X)-\tilde{K}(\cdot|X))\|_{\mathrm{TV}}
, combined with what is assumed in this lemma, we obtain the result.
∎

A.3
Step 3: Bounding the Chi-square divergence using the Brascamp-Lieb inequality

We proceed with lower bounding (
S.16
), where in a slight abuse of notation, we shall denote
\breve{K}^{j}
by
K^{j}
for the remainder of the section.

To lower bound the Bayes risk, in light of the Neyman-Pearson lemma, it should suffice to show that
{\mathcal{L}}^{Y|U=u}_{\pi}(Y)
is close to
1
with high probability. This is made precise below by showing that the right-hand side of (
S.16
) is further bounded from below by

1-\left(\sqrt{(1/2)\int{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}\left({\mathcal{L}}^{Y|U=u}_{\pi}(Y)-1\right)^{2}d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u)}-\alpha/2\right).

(S.18)

To see this, note that any
T:{\mathcal{Y}}^{m}\to\{0,1\}
, we can write
A_{T}=T^{-1}(\{0\})
and note that

\displaystyle P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}T(Y)+P_{\pi}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}(1-T(Y))

\displaystyle=1-\left(P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}\left(Y\in A_{T}\right)-P_{\pi}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}\left(Y\in A_{T}\right)\right).

We obtain that

\displaystyle\int\left(P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}T+\int P_{f}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}(1-T)d\pi\circ\Psi^{-1}(f)\right)d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\underset{A}{\sup}\,|\int P_{0}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}(A)-P_{\pi}^{m}\tilde{K}_{u}(A)d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u)|.

We find by Jensen’s inequality that

\|{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y}-{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\int\|{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}-{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y|U=u}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u).

Combining the above with Pinsker’s second inequality a standard bound for the Kullback-Leibler divergence (see Lemma
35
), we obtain (
S.18
).

This brings us to a crucial part of the proof; the application of Lemma 10.1 in
[
77
]
, which we restate as Lemma
10
below. We first introduce some notation.

We note that for
f\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}}
, it holds that

\frac{dP_{\Psi f}}{dP_{0}}(X^{(j)}_{i})\overset{d}{=}\frac{dN\left({f},\sigma^{2}I_{d_{L}}\right)}{dN\left(0,\sigma^{2}I_{d_{L}}\right)}=:\mathscr{L}_{f}^{ji}(X^{(j)}_{i}),

where the equality in distribution is true under
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{X}
.

Denote the “local” and “global” likelihoods of the data as

\mathscr{L}_{f}^{j}(X^{(j)})={\prod}_{i=1}^{n}\mathscr{L}_{f}^{ji}(X^{(j)}_{i}),\;\;\;\mathscr{L}_{f}(X):={\prod}_{j=1}^{m}\mathscr{L}_{f}^{j}(X^{(j)}),

and the mixture likelihoods as

\mathscr{L}_{\pi}^{j}(X)=\int\mathscr{L}_{f}(X^{(j)})d\pi(f)\;\text{ and }\;\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(X)=\int\mathscr{L}_{f}(X)d\pi(f).

In view of the Markov chain structure, the probability measure
d{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}(x,u,y)
disintegrates as
d{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|(X,U)=(x,u)}d{\mathbb{P}}^{X}_{f}(x)d{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(u)d\pi(f)
. Using this,
{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}\left(L^{Y|U=u}_{\pi}(Y)\right)^{2}
can be seen to equal

{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(X)\bigg|Y,U=u\right]^{2}=\int\left(\int\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x)\frac{dK(\cdot|x,u)}{d{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|U=u}_{0}}(y)d{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{X}(x)\right)^{2}d{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|U=u}_{0}(y),

(S.19)

where it is used that
K(\cdot|x,u)\ll{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|U=u}_{0}(\cdot)
,
{\mathbb{P}}^{(X,U)}_{f}
-almost surely (Lemma
39
). Using Fubini’s theorem (“decoupling” in
X
), we can write the above display as

\int\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x_{1})\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x_{2})q_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})d({\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{X}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{X})(x_{1},x_{2}),

(S.20)

where

q_{u}(x_{1},x_{2}):=\int\frac{dK(\cdot|x_{1},u)}{d{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}}(y)\frac{dK(\cdot|x_{2},u)}{d{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}}(y)d{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|U=u}_{0}(y).

(S.21)

Since
K(\cdot|x,u)
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|U=u}_{0}
are product measures on
{\mathcal{Y}}={\mathcal{Y}}^{m}
, we can write
q_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})=\Pi_{j=1}^{m}q_{u}^{j}(x_{1},x_{2})
where

q_{u}^{j}(x_{1},x_{2})=\int\frac{K^{j}(y^{j}|x_{1}^{j},u)K^{j}(y^{j}|x_{2}^{j},u)}{{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y^{j}|U=u}(y^{j})}d{\mathbb{P}}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}_{0}(y).

(S.22)

The map
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto q_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})
can be seen as capturing the dependence between the original data
X
and a random variable
X^{\prime}
with conditional distribution

X^{\prime}|X=x\sim\int d{\mathbb{P}}^{X|(Y,U)=(y,u)}_{0}d{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|(X,U)=(x,u)},

(S.23)

which is sometimes referred to as the “forward-backward channel”, stemming from the fact that
X\to Y\to X^{\prime}
forms a Markov chain. An easy computation using the law of total expectation shows that the covariance of
q_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})d(P_{0}\times P_{0})(x_{1},x_{2})
,

\int\left(\begin{matrix}x_{1}\\
x_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\left(\begin{matrix}x_{1}^{\top}&x_{2}^{\top}\end{matrix}\right)q_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})d(P_{0}\times P_{0})(x_{1},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2mnd_{L}\times 2mnd_{L}},

(S.24)

is equal to
\Sigma_{u}:=\text{Diag}\left(\Sigma^{11}_{u},\dots,\Sigma^{1n}_{u},\dots,\Sigma^{m1}_{u},\dots,\Sigma^{mn}_{u}\right)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2mnd_{L}\times 2mnd_{L}}
for

\Sigma^{ji}:=\sigma^{2}\left(\begin{matrix}I_{d_{L}}&\Xi_{u}^{ji}\\
\Xi_{u}^{ji}&I_{d_{L}}\end{matrix}\right),

with

\Xi_{u}^{ji}:={\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\sigma^{-1}{X}^{(j)}_{L;i}\bigg|Y^{(j)},U=u\right]{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\sigma^{-1}{X}^{(j)}_{L;i}\bigg|Y^{(j)},U=u\right]^{\top}.

Define also

\Xi_{u}^{j}:={\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\sigma^{-1}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}{X}^{(j)}_{L;i}\bigg|Y^{(j)},U=u\right]{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\sigma^{-1}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}{X}^{(j)}_{L;i}\bigg|Y^{(j)},U=u\right]^{\top}.

(S.25)

We are now ready to state the lemma that forms the crux of our distributed testing lower bound proof.

Lemma 10
.

Suppose that
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto q_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})
is bounded and that
\pi
is a centered Gaussian distribution on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Then,

\frac{\int\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x_{1})\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x_{2})q_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})d({\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{X}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{X})(x_{1},x_{2})}{\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}\int\mathscr{L}^{j}_{\pi}(x_{1}^{j})\mathscr{L}^{j}_{\pi}(x_{2}^{j})q_{u}^{j}(x_{1}^{j},x_{2}^{j})d({\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{X^{(j)}}\times{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{X^{(j)}})(x_{1}^{j},x_{2}^{j})}

(S.26)

is bounded above by

\frac{\int\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x_{1})\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x_{2})dN(0,\Sigma)(x_{1},x_{2})}{\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}\int\mathscr{L}^{j}_{\pi}(x_{1}^{j})\mathscr{L}^{j}_{\pi}(x_{2}^{j})dN(0,\Sigma^{j})(x_{1}^{j},x_{2}^{j})}.

The lemma has the following interpretation: the ratio of the second moment of the Bayes factor of the “global Bayesian hypothesis test” that of the product of second moments of the “local Bayes factors”, is maximized over the class of forward-backward channel with covariance
\Sigma
when the forward-backward channel is Gaussian.

There is an existing literature on Brascamp-Lieb inequality in relation to information theoretical problems, in relation to mutual information
[
28
,
61
,
62
]
. For a proof, we refer to Lemma 10.1 in
[
77
]
, which uses that the prior
\pi
is Gaussian, exploiting the conjugacy between the prior and the model which enables the use of techniques from
[
60
]
.

The main consequence of the above lemma is analytic expressions, which can be used to upper bound the chi-square divergence in (
S.18
) which is the content of Lemma
11
below.

Lemma 11
.

Define

\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}:=\int e^{f^{\top}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Xi^{j}_{u}g}d(\pi\times\pi)(f,g)

(S.27)

and

\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}:=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{{Y^{(j)}}|U=u}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\mathscr{L}_{\pi}\left({X}^{{(j)}}\right)\bigg|Y^{{(j)}},U=u\right]^{2}.

(S.28)

If
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto q_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})
is bounded and if
\pi
is a centered Gaussian distribution on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}}
, it holds that

{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}\left(L^{Y|U=u}_{\pi}(Y)\right)^{2}\leq\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}\cdot\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}.

The above lemma describes how the variance of the Bayes factor given
U
is bounded by two factors. One factor depends on the Fisher information of the transcript’s likelihood at
f=0
given
U=u
;
\Xi_{u}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Xi^{j}_{u}
. In this sense,
\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}
captures how well the transcript allows for “estimation” of
f
. The second factor can be seen as the
m
-fold product of the local Bayes factors, capturing essentially the power of combining the locally most powerful test statistics; the likelihood ratios.

We start by noting that
\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}
is equal to the denominator of (
S.26
). By Lemma
10
,

{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}\left(L^{Y|U=u}_{\pi}(Y)\right)^{2}\leq\frac{\int\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x_{1})\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x_{2})dN(0,\Sigma)(x_{1},x_{2})}{\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}\int\mathscr{L}^{j}_{\pi}(x_{1}^{j})\mathscr{L}^{j}_{\pi}(x_{2}^{j})dN(0,\Sigma^{j})(x_{1}^{j},x_{2}^{j})}\cdot\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}.

By the block diagonal matrix structure of
\Sigma
, the denominator in the first factor of the right-hand side satisfies

\displaystyle\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}\int\mathscr{L}^{j}_{\pi}(x_{1}^{j})\mathscr{L}^{j}_{\pi}(x_{2}^{j})dN(0,\Sigma^{j})(x_{1}^{j},x_{2}^{j})

\displaystyle=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}\int e^{\frac{1}{2}\left(\|\sqrt{\Sigma^{j}}\left({f},{g}\right)\|_{2}^{2}-\|\left({f},{g}\right)\|_{2}^{2}\right)}d(\pi\times\pi)\left({f},{g}\right)

\displaystyle=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}\int e^{f^{\top}\Xi^{j}_{u}g}d(\pi\times\pi)(f,g)

\displaystyle\geq\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}e^{\int f^{\top}\Xi^{j}_{u}g\,d(\pi\times\pi)(f,g)}=1.

Through the expression for the moment generating function of the Gaussian, the numerator of
\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}
is equal to

\int\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x_{1})\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(x_{2})dN(0,\Sigma)(x_{1},x_{2})=\int e^{f^{\top}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Xi^{j}_{u}g}d(\pi\times\pi)(f,g).

∎

A.4
Step 4: Adversarially choosing the prior based on shared or local randomness

Suppose that for some constant
c>0
,

{\varrho^{2}}\|\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}}^{\top}\Xi_{u}\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}}\|\leq c.

(S.29)

If
\bar{\Gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}\times d_{L}}
is symmetric, idempotent with rank proportional to
d_{L}
and
\pi=N(0,\varrho^{2}\bar{\Gamma})
, standard results for the Gaussian chaos, e.g. Lemma 6.2.2 in
[
83
]
combined with (
S.29
) and the fact that
\|\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}}\|\leq 1
, yield that

\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}\leq\exp\left({C\sigma^{-2}{\varrho^{4}}\text{Tr}\left((\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}}^{\top}\Xi_{u}\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}})^{2}\right)}\right),

for a constant
C>0
depending only on
c
. As a final step of the testing risk lower bound technique, we use essentially a geometric argument to sharpen this bound in case the distributed protocol does not enjoy shared randomness. The
d_{L}\times d_{L}
matrix
\Xi_{u}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\Xi_{u}^{j}
geometrically captures how well
Y
allows to “reconstruct” the compressed sample
X
. When
U
is degenerate,
\Xi_{u}
is “known” to the prior, and
\bar{\Gamma}
can be chosen to exploit “direction” in which
\Xi_{u}
contains the least information. We finish this section by proving the lemma below, which makes this notion precise.

Lemma 12
.

Let
\alpha\in(0,1)
and suppose that the map
(x_{1},x_{2})\mapsto q_{u}(x_{1},x_{2})
defined in (
S.21
) is bounded for all distributed testing protocols in
\mathscr{T}
. Let
\pi=N(0,\varrho^{2}\bar{\Gamma})
, with
\varrho:=\frac{\rho}{c_{\alpha}^{1/4}d^{1/2}_{L}}
and
\bar{\Gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}\times d_{L}}
is symmetric, idempotent with rank proportional to
d_{L}
. Assume that
\rho
is such that
\varrho^{2}\|\Xi_{u}\|\leq c

{\mathbb{P}}^{U}
-a.s. for some constant
c>0
. It then holds that

\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}\leq\exp\left({C\sigma^{-2}\frac{\rho^{4}}{c_{\alpha}d^{2}_{L}}\|\Xi_{u}\|\text{Tr}\left(\Xi_{u}\right)}\right),

(S.30)

for some fixed constant
C>0
depending only on
c>0
. Furthermore, if
U
is degenerate and
\frac{2\rho^{2}}{\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}d^{2}}\text{Tr}(\Xi)\leq c
, it holds that

\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}\leq\exp\left({C\sigma^{-2}\frac{\rho^{4}}{c_{\alpha}d^{3}_{L}}\text{Tr}\left(\Xi_{u}\right)^{2}}\right),

(S.31)

for some fixed constant
C>0
depending only on
c>0
.

In case of shared randomness (i.e.
U
not being degenerate), simply taking
\bar{\Gamma}=I_{d_{L}}
, noting that
\text{Tr}(\Xi^{2}_{u})=\|\Xi_{u}\|\,\text{Tr}(\Xi_{u})
and following the argument earlier in the section yields (
S.30
).

Now assume
U
is degenerate and write
\Xi_{u}=\Xi
. The matrix
\Xi
is positive definite and symmetric, therefore it possesses a spectral decomposition
V^{\top}\text{Diag}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{d_{L}})V
. Without loss of generality, assume that
\xi_{1}\geq\xi_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\xi_{d_{L}}
with corresponding eigenvectors
V=\left(\begin{matrix}v_{1}&\cdots&v_{d_{L}}\end{matrix}\right)
. Let
\check{V}
denote the
{d_{L}}\times\lceil{d_{L}}/2\rceil
matrix
\left(\begin{matrix}v_{\lfloor{d_{L}}/2\rfloor+1}&\cdots&v_{d_{L}}\end{matrix}\right)
. The choice of prior may depend on
\Xi
, to see this, note the order of the supremum and infimum in (
S.18
) and the fact that
\Xi
solely depends on the choice of kernel. To that extent, set
\bar{\Gamma}=\check{V}\check{V}^{\top}
. It holds that

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(\check{V}\check{V}^{\top})

\displaystyle=\underset{i=1}{\overset{d_{L}}{\sum}}\underset{k=\lfloor{d_{L}}/2\rfloor+1}{\overset{d_{L}}{\sum}}(v_{k})_{i}^{2}=\lceil{d_{L}}/2\rceil.

The choice
\bar{\Gamma}
is thus seen to satisfy the conditions of symmetry and positive definiteness and is idempotent with rank
\lceil{d_{L}}/2\rceil
.

Since the eigenvalues are decreasingly ordered,

\xi_{\lfloor{d_{L}}/2\rfloor}\leq\frac{2}{{d_{L}}}\underset{i=1}{\overset{\lfloor{d_{L}}/2\rfloor}{\sum}}\xi_{i}\leq\frac{2}{{d_{L}}}\text{Tr}(\Xi).

By orthogonality of the columns of
V
,
\check{V}^{\top}\Xi\check{V}=\text{Diag}(\xi_{\lfloor d/2\rfloor+1},\dots,\xi_{d_{L}})
. The condition of (
S.29
) reduces to

\displaystyle\varrho^{2}\|\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}}^{\top}\Xi_{u}\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}}\|

\displaystyle\leq\varrho^{2}\xi_{\lfloor{d_{L}}/2\rfloor}\leq 2\frac{\rho^{2}}{\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}{d_{L}}^{2}}\text{Tr}(\Xi).

Furthermore,

\displaystyle\text{Tr}\big((\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}}^{\top}\Xi_{u}\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}})^{2}\big)=\text{Tr}\left((\check{V}^{\top}\Xi\check{V})^{2}\right)=\underset{i=\lfloor{d_{L}}/2\rfloor+1}{\overset{{d_{L}}}{\sum}}\xi_{i}^{2}\leq{d_{L}}\xi_{\lfloor{d_{L}}/2\rfloor}^{2}\leq\frac{4}{{d_{L}}}\text{Tr}(\Xi)^{2},

which implies in turn that

\varrho^{4}\text{Tr}\left((\check{V}^{\top}\Xi\check{V})^{2}\right)\leq 4\frac{\rho^{4}}{c_{\alpha}{d_{L}}^{3}}\text{Tr}(\Xi)^{2}.

∎

A.5
Step 5: Capturing the cost of privacy in trace of
\Xi_{u}
and the local Bayes factors

The cost of privacy is captured through bounds on
\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}
and
\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}
. These bounds specifically use the fact that the Markov kernels that underlie these quantities are
(\epsilon,6\delta)
-differentially private.

We start with the bound on
\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}
, for which we proceed by a data processing argument for the matrix
\Xi_{u}
under the
(\epsilon,6\delta)
-DP constraint. This comes in the guise of Lemma
13
below. Its proof is deferred to the end of the section.

Lemma 13
.

Let
0<\epsilon\leq 1
and let
Y^{(j)}
be a transcript generated by an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP constraint distributed protocol, with
0<\epsilon\leq 1
and
0\leq\delta\leq\left(\left({n}{{d_{L}}}^{-1}\wedge n^{1/2}{d_{L}}^{-1/2}\right)\epsilon^{2}\right)^{1+\omega}
for some
\omega>0
. The matrix
\Xi_{u}^{j}
as defined in (
S.25
) satisfies

\text{Tr}\left(\Xi_{u}^{j}\right)\leq(Cn^{2}\epsilon^{2})\wedge(nd_{L})

for a fixed constant
C>0
. Furthermore, it holds that
\Xi_{u}^{j}\leq nI_{d_{L}}
.

The lemma implies in particular that
\|\Xi_{u}^{j}\|\leq(Cn^{2}\epsilon^{2})\wedge n
, as
\Xi_{u}^{j}
is symmetric and positive definite. Combining this with (
S.9
) and the triangle inequality, we obtain

{\varrho^{2}}\|\Xi_{u}\|\leq{\varrho^{2}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}\|\Xi_{u}^{j}\|\leq\frac{m\left((Cn^{2}\epsilon^{2})\wedge n\right)\rho^{2}}{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}d_{L}}\leq C\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}.

(S.32)

Similarly, (
S.8
) yields

\frac{2\rho^{2}}{\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}d^{2}_{L}}\text{Tr}\left(\Xi_{u}^{j}\right)\leq\frac{m\left((Cn^{2}\epsilon^{2})\wedge(nd_{L})\right)\rho^{2}}{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}d^{2}_{L}}\leq C\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}/\sqrt{d_{L}}.

(S.33)

The last two displays together finish the verification of the conditions of Lemma
12
. The above data processing inequalities for
\Xi_{u}^{j}
and bounds on
\rho^{2}
also yield a bound on
\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}
as defined in Lemma
12
. In case of shared randomness protocols, using (
S.30
), (
S.32
), Lemma
13
and (
S.9
), we obtain

\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}\leq\exp\left(C^{2}c_{\alpha}\right).

In case of local randomness protocols, combining (
S.31
) with (
S.33
) and (
S.8
) yields the above bound on
\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}
.

Next, we turn to
\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}
. The first bound, given in Lemma
14
, does not use privacy at all. This bound is only tight whenever
\epsilon\gtrsim 1/\sqrt{n}
(as a bound on
\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}
), in which case it corresponds to the regime where majority voting bares no privacy cost.

Lemma 14
.

Consider
\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}
as in (
S.28
) with
\pi=N(0,\rho^{2}c_{\alpha}^{-1/2}d^{-1}_{L}\bar{\Gamma})
with
\bar{\Gamma}
idempotent. It holds that

\mathrm{B}_{u}^{\pi}\leq\exp\left(C\frac{mn^{2}\rho^{4}}{c_{\alpha}\sigma^{2}d_{L}}\right).

Since conditional expectation contracts the
L_{2}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y})
-norm,

\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{{Y^{(j)}}|U=u}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\mathscr{L}_{\pi}\left({X}^{{(j)}}\right)\bigg|Y^{{(j)}},U=u\right]^{2}\leq\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{X^{(j)}}\left[\mathscr{L}_{\pi}\left({X}^{(j)}\right)^{2}\right].

We now proceed to bound the first factor in the product on the right-hand side of the display above, which for a positive semi-definite choice of
\bar{\Gamma}
equals

\int{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{X^{(j)}}\exp\left((\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}}(f+g))^{\top}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{X}^{{(j)}}_{L;i}-\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\|\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}}f\right\|_{2}^{2}-\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\|\sqrt{\bar{\Gamma}}g\right\|_{2}^{2}\right)dN\left(0,\frac{\rho^{2}}{\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}d_{L}}I_{2d_{L}}\right)(f,g).

By direct computation involving the moment generating function of the normal distribution, the latter display equals

\int\exp\left(\frac{n\rho^{2}}{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}d_{L}}z^{\top}\bar{\Gamma}z^{\prime}\right)dN(0,I_{2d_{L}})(z,z^{\prime}).

We aim to apply the moment generating function of the Gaussian chaos, e.g. Lemma 6.2.2 in
[
83
]
to the above display. Using that
\rho^{2}
satisfies (
S.8
) or (
S.9
) and since
\|\bar{\Gamma}\|=1
by the fact that
\bar{\Gamma}
is idempotent,
\frac{n\rho^{2}}{\sigma^{2}c_{\alpha}^{1/2}{d_{L}}}\leq\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}
with
c_{\alpha}>0
chosen small enough, the aforementioned result yields that there exists a constant
C>0

\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{X^{(j)}|U=u}\left[\mathscr{L}_{\pi}\left({X}^{(j)}\right)^{2}\right]\leq\exp\left(Cc_{\alpha}^{-1}\frac{mn^{2}\rho^{4}}{\sigma^{4}d_{L}}\right),

(S.34)

where
C>0
is universal.
∎

Whenever
\epsilon\leq n^{-1/2}
, a much more involved data processing argument is needed than the one above. In the argument that follows, we will use the bound on
{\mathcal{L}}_{\pi,u}^{j}
obtained through Lemma
7
in Step 2. The data processing argument leads to the bound of Lemma
15
below. Its proof is based on coupling arguments, where the two different couplings constructed result in the different rates observed in the condition of the theorem.

Lemma 15
.

Let
\pi=N(0,d^{-1}_{L}\rho^{2}\bar{\Gamma})
, with
\bar{\Gamma}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}\times d_{L}}
a symmetric idempotent matrix,

\rho^{2}\leq\sigma^{2}c_{\alpha}({d^{1/2}_{L}}/({\sqrt{m}n^{\frac{3}{2}}\epsilon})\vee{1}/{(mn^{2}\epsilon^{2})})

and
\{K^{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
correspond to a
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP distributed protocol with transcripts
Y^{(j)}
such that
0<\epsilon\leq 1
,
\delta\lesssim c_{\alpha}(m^{-1}\wedge\epsilon)
and

|{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\pi,u}^{j}(y)-1|\leq\frac{5m^{1/2}}{{\alpha}}\;\;\;P_{0}K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)\text{-a.s.}

Then, there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

\mathrm{B}^{\pi}_{u}\leq e^{C\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}}.

(S.35)

Combining the lemma above with the bound
\mathrm{B}^{\pi}_{u}\leq\exp(C\frac{mn^{2}\rho^{4}}{\sigma^{2}d_{L}})
(which follows from Lemma
14
), we obtain that (
S.35
) holds whenever
\rho
satisfies (
S.8
) or (
S.9
). Combining this with the bounds on
\mathrm{A}_{u}^{\pi}
derived earlier and considering (
S.18
) lower bounds the testing risk, we obtain that

{\mathcal{R}}(H_{\rho}^{{s},R},T)>1-\alpha

for
c_{\alpha}>0
small enough, from which the result of Theorem
5
follows. To complete the proof, we provide the proofs of Lemmas
13
and
15
in the following subsection.

A.5.1
Proof of Lemma
15

Before providing the proof of Lemma
15
, we first develop additional tools.

First off is the following general coupling lemma that will be used in the proof of Lemma
15
. The lemma is in essence Lemma 6.1 in
[
51
]
, but its proof might be easier to verify and is provided for completeness.

Lemma 16
.

Consider random variables
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P_{1}
and
\tilde{X}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}P_{2}
defined on the same space. Write
X=(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n})
,
\tilde{X}=(\tilde{X}_{1},\ldots,\tilde{X}_{n})
and let
K
be a Markov kernel between the sample space of
X
(equivalently
\tilde{X}
) and an arbitrary target space, satisfying an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP constraint (i.e. (
S.2
)) with
\epsilon\leq 1
.

Suppose that there exists a coupling
{\mathbb{P}}
of
(\tilde{X},{X})
such that
{\mathbb{P}}^{\tilde{X}}=P_{1}^{n}
,
{\mathbb{P}}^{X}=P_{2}^{n}
and

D_{i}:=\text{1}\left\{\tilde{X}_{i}\neq{X}_{i}\right\}\sim\text{Ber}\left(p\right),\text{ i.i.d. for }i=1,\dots,n,\;p\in[0,1]

under
{\mathbb{P}}
.

Then, it holds that

P_{1}^{n}K\left(A|\tilde{X}\right)\leq e^{4\epsilon np}P_{2}^{n}K\left(A|{X}\right)+2\delta e^{4np\epsilon}.

(S.36)

Let
{\mathbb{E}}
denote expectation with respect to
{\mathbb{P}}
and write
D=(D_{i})_{i\in[n]}
,
S:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}
. We start by noting that

{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=0\right]={\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|{X})|S=0\right].

(S.37)

Next, we show that for all
k\in[n]
,

\displaystyle e^{-\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=k-1\right]-\delta\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=k\right]\leq e^{\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=k-1\right]+\delta.

(S.38)

Write
v_{(-i)}=(v_{i})_{[n]\setminus\{i\}}
for a vector
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Let
k\in[n]
be given and let
{\mathcal{V}}_{k}
denote the set of
v\in\{0,1\}^{n}
’s such that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}v_{i}=k
. Using the definition of DP, the integrand in the conditional expectation satisfies

e^{-\epsilon}K(A|\tilde{X}_{1},\dots,\breve{X}_{i},\dots,\tilde{X}_{n})-\delta\leq K(A|\tilde{X})\leq e^{\epsilon}K(A|\tilde{X}_{1},\dots,\breve{X}_{i},\dots,\tilde{X}_{n})+\delta,

(S.39)

for any random variable
\breve{X}_{i}
taking values in the sample space of
\tilde{X}_{i}
. In particular, if
v_{i}=1
it holds that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|x_{1},\dots,{X}_{i},\dots,x_{n})\big|D_{i}=v_{i},\tilde{X}_{(-i)}=x_{(-i)},D_{(-i)}=v_{(-i)}\right]\leq

\displaystyle e^{\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|x_{1},\dots,{X}_{i},\dots,x_{n})\big|D_{i}=0,\tilde{X}_{(-i)}=x_{(-i)},D_{(-i)}=v_{(-i)}\right]+\delta,

for all
x
in the sample space of
\tilde{X}
. It follows by the law of total probability that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|D=v\right]\leq e^{\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|D_{i}=0,\,D_{k}=v_{k}\text{ for }k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}\right]+\delta,

for all
i\in[n]
. The event
\left\{D=v\right\}
equals the event
\left\{D=v,\,S=k\right\}
, and similarly it holds that

\left\{D_{k}=v_{k}\text{ for }k\in[n]\setminus\{i\},\,D_{i}=0\right\}=\left\{D_{k}=v_{k}\text{ for }k\in[n]\setminus\{i\},\,D_{i}=0,\,S=k-1\right\}.

Consider now the sets

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}_{k-1}(v)

\displaystyle:=\left\{v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k-1}\,:\,v_{k}=v_{k}^{\prime}\text{ except for one }i\in[n]\right\}\text{ for }v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}_{k}(v^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=\left\{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k}\,:\,v_{k}=v_{k}^{\prime}\text{ except for one }i\in[n]\right\}\text{ for }v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k-1}.

By what we have derived so far, it holds that any
v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k}
and
v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k-1}(v)
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|D=v,\,S=k\right]\leq e^{\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|D=v^{\prime},\,S=k-1\right]+\delta.

Consider
\{I_{k}(v):v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k}\}
independent random variables (on a possibly enlarged probability space) taking values in
[n]
such that
{\mathbb{P}}(I_{k}(v)=i)=1/k
whenever
v_{i}=1
. Combining the above with the total law of probability we find that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=k\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{n\choose k}}\underset{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k}}{\overset{}{\sum}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|D=v,\,S=k\right]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle e^{\epsilon}\frac{1}{{n\choose k}}\underset{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k}}{\overset{}{\sum}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|D_{I(v)}=0,\,D_{-I(v)}=v_{-I(v)},\,S=k-1\right]+\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\epsilon}\frac{1}{{n\choose k}}\frac{1}{k}\underset{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\underset{v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k-1}(v)}{\overset{}{\sum}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|D=v^{\prime},\,S=k-1\right]+\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\epsilon}\frac{1}{{n\choose k}}\frac{1}{k}\underset{v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k-1}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\underset{v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k}(v^{\prime})}{\overset{}{\sum}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|D=v^{\prime},\,S=k-1\right]+\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\epsilon}\frac{1}{{n\choose k-1}}\underset{v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k-1}}{\overset{}{\sum}}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|D=v^{\prime},\,S=k-1\right]+\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=k-1\right]+\delta,

where it is used that
|{\mathcal{V}}_{k}|={n\choose k}
,

{\mathbb{P}}(D_{1}=v_{1},\dots,D_{n}=v_{n}|S=k)={\mathbb{P}}(D_{1}=\tilde{v}_{1},\dots,D_{n}=\tilde{v}_{n}|S=k)

for all
v=(v_{i})_{i\in[n]},\tilde{v}=(\tilde{v}_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k}
and for any
v^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k-1}
there are
n-k+1
ways to obtain
v\in{\mathcal{V}}_{k}
such that
v_{k}=v_{k}^{\prime}
except for one
i\in[n]
.

By applying the privacy lower bound of (
S.39
) and repeating the same steps, we also find that

e^{-\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=k-1\right]-\delta\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=k\right].

This proves (
S.38
), which, applying iteratively, results in the bound

\displaystyle e^{-\epsilon k}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=0\right]-\delta k\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=k\right]\leq e^{\epsilon k}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=0\right]+\delta ke^{\epsilon k},

(S.40)

for
k=0,1,\dots,n
. By symmetry of the argument, the same inequalities hold for
{X}
in place of
\tilde{X}
. Using the above inequalities, we can bound

P_{1}K(A|\tilde{X})={\mathbb{E}}K(A|\tilde{X})={\mathbb{E}}^{S}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S\right],

by

{\mathbb{E}}^{S}e^{S\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|\tilde{X})|S=0\right]+\delta{\mathbb{E}}Se^{S\epsilon}.

Similarly, applying (
S.38
) with
{X}
in place of
\tilde{X}
, we find

\displaystyle P_{2}K(A|{X})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{S}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|{X})|S\right]

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{E}}^{S}e^{-S\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}\left[K(A|{X})|S=0\right]-{\mathbb{E}}\delta S.

(S.41)

Combining the two inequalities with (
S.37
), we obtain that

P_{1}K(A|\tilde{X})\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}^{S}e^{S\epsilon}}{{\mathbb{E}}^{S}e^{-S\epsilon}}\left(P_{2}K(A|{X})+{\mathbb{E}}^{S}\delta S\right)+\delta{\mathbb{E}}Se^{S\epsilon}.

(S.42)

In view of the moment generating function of the binomial distribution,

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathbb{E}}^{S}e^{S\epsilon}}{{\mathbb{E}}^{S}e^{-S\epsilon}}=\left(\frac{1+p(e^{\epsilon}-1)}{1+p(e^{-\epsilon}-1)}\right)^{n}\leq e^{4np\epsilon},

where the inequality follows from
0\leq\epsilon,p\leq 1
, the inequality
e^{x}-e^{-x}\leq 3x
for
0\leq x\leq 1
and Taylor expanding
\log(1+x)=x-{x^{2}}/{2}+\ldots
. By Chebyshev’s association inequality (e.g. Theorem 2.14 in
[
15
]
),
{\mathbb{E}}^{S}S{\mathbb{E}}^{S}e^{S\epsilon}\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{S}Se^{S\epsilon}
. Consequently, using the nonnegativity of
S
,

\displaystyle\delta\left(\frac{{\mathbb{E}}^{S}e^{S\epsilon}}{{\mathbb{E}}^{S}e^{-S\epsilon}}{\mathbb{E}}^{S}S+{\mathbb{E}}Se^{S\epsilon}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta{\mathbb{E}}Se^{S\epsilon}.

Lemma
38
(a straightforward calculation) now finishes the proof.
∎

Next, we construct the coupling that will be used in the proof of Lemma
15
, in conjunction with Lemma
16
above. The couplings use similar ideas to the one constructed in
[
65
]
.

Lemma 17
.

Let
K^{j}
satisfy an
(\epsilon,\delta)
-DP constraint for
0<\epsilon\leq 1
.

Consider
\pi=N(0,c_{\alpha}^{1/2}d^{-1}_{L}\rho^{2}\bar{\Gamma})
, with
\rho^{2}
satisfying (
S.8
) or (
S.9
), with
\epsilon\leq 1/\sqrt{n}
and
\delta\leq c_{\alpha}(m^{-1}\wedge\epsilon)
.

For all measurable sets
A
it holds that

P_{\pi}K^{j}\left(A|X^{(j)},u\right)\leq\left(1+c_{\alpha}^{1/4}m^{-1/2}\right)P_{0}K^{j}\left(A|X^{(j)},u\right)+2\delta+\frac{c_{\alpha}}{m^{3/2}}

(S.43)

and

P_{\pi}K^{j}\left(A|X^{(j)},u\right)\geq\left(1-c_{\alpha}^{1/4}m^{-1/2}\right)P_{0}K^{j}\left(A|X^{(j)},u\right)-2\delta-\frac{c_{\alpha}}{m^{3/2}}

(S.44)

for all
c_{\alpha}>0
small enough.

We suppress the dependence of the Markov kernels on the draw of the shared randomness
u
, as it is of no relevance to the proof. Consider
\tilde{X}^{(j)}\sim P_{\pi}
and
{X}^{(j)}\sim P_{0}
. Since
{X}^{(j)}_{L^{\prime}k}\overset{d}{=}\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L^{\prime}k}
for all
L^{\prime}>L
, it suffices to construct couplings for
(\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L},{X}^{(j)}_{L})
. We construct two couplings, one for each of the different regimes of
\epsilon
. That is, for each of the regimes, we derive a joint distribution of
(\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L},{X}^{(j)}_{L})
called
{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi,0}
such that
\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L}\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi,0}^{\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L}}=P_{\pi}
and
{X}^{(j)}_{L}\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi,0}^{X^{(j)}_{L}}=P_{0}
. The specific couplings that we construct aim at assuring
\mathrm{d}_{H}(\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L},{X}^{(j)}_{L})
is small with high probability. When constructing the coupling below, it suffices to consider
\sigma=1
without loss of generality (i.e. by rescaling
X^{(j)}
and
\tilde{X}^{(j)}
). After the construction of both the couplings, the lemma follows by an application of Lemma
16
.

Case 1:
Consider
1/\sqrt{n}\geq\epsilon\geq{1}/{\sqrt{m{nd_{L}}}}
. The construction we follow is similar to that used in Theorem D.6 of
[
66
]
, whose dependencies are favorable for our purpose.

If
n=1
, Pinsker’s inequality followed Lemma
35
and Lemma
14
applied with
m=1
yield that

\displaystyle\|P_{0}-P_{\pi}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}D_{\chi^{2}}(P_{0};P_{\pi})}\leq C\frac{\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}\rho^{2}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}

for a universal constant
C>0
contingent only on
\|\bar{\Gamma}\|
. By Lemma
32
, there exists a coupling
{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi,0}
such that
\tilde{X}^{(j)}\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi,0}^{\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L}}=P_{\pi}
and
{X}^{(j)}\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi,0}^{X^{(j)}_{L}}=P_{0}
and

p:={\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L}\neq{X}^{(j)}_{L}\right)\leq\left(C\frac{\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}\rho^{2}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\right)\wedge 1.

Applying Lemma
16
, it follows that

\displaystyle P_{\pi}^{n}K^{j}(A|\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\pi,0}K^{j}(A|\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L})

\displaystyle\leq e^{4\epsilon np}P_{0}K^{j}(A|{X}^{(j)}_{L})+2\delta npe^{\epsilon np}.

By applying condition (
S.8
) or (
S.9
) and the bound on
p
, we obtain that

e^{C\frac{\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}\epsilon\rho^{2}}{d^{1/2}_{L}}}\leq 1+C\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}/\sqrt{m}

Similarly, using that
\delta\leq\epsilon/\sqrt{m}
,

\displaystyle\delta pe^{\epsilon p}\leq\delta+C^{\prime}c_{\alpha}/m^{3/2}.

The first identity we wish to show, i.e. (
S.43
), now follows for
n=1
and a sufficiently small enough choice of
c_{\alpha}>0
.

For what follows, take
n>1
. Consider
V
a uniform draw from the unit sphere in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}}
and
Z\sim N(0,I_{d_{L}})
, both independent of the other random variables considered. We have

\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}:=\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}X^{(j)}_{L;i}\overset{d}{=}\frac{\|Z\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n}}V

for
{X}^{(j)}_{L}\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{X^{(j)}_{L}}
(see e.g.
[
83
]
Exercise 3.3.7). Similarly,

\overline{\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L}}\overset{d}{=}\frac{\left\|(I_{d_{L}}+\frac{nc_{\alpha}^{1/2}\rho^{2}}{d_{L}}\bar{\Gamma})^{1/2}Z\right\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n}}V.

Next, we note that for
\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{n}\sim N(0,I_{d_{L}})
independent of
X^{(j)}=({X}^{(j)}_{1},\dots,{X}^{(j)}_{n})
, we have

X^{(j)}_{L}\overset{d}{=}\left(\overline{X^{(j)}}+\eta_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\eta_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq n}.

(S.45)

To see this, note that both the left- and right-hand side are mean zero Gaussian and

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left(\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}+\eta_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\eta_{i}\right)\left(\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}+\eta_{k}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\eta_{i}\right)^{\top}

\displaystyle=

(S.46)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}I_{d_{L}}+\text{1}_{i=k}I_{d}-\frac{2}{n}I_{d}+\frac{1}{n}I_{d_{L}}

\displaystyle=\text{1}_{i=k}I_{d_{L}},

which means that the covariances of the left-hand side and right-hand side of (
S.45
) are equal too. Noting that
\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L}\overset{d}{=}(F+X^{(j)}_{L;i})_{i\in[n]}
and
\overline{\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L}}\overset{d}{=}F+\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}
, where
F\sim N(0,\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}d^{-1}_{L}\rho^{2}\bar{\Gamma})
is independent of
\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L}\overset{d}{=}\left(\overline{\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L}}+\eta_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\eta_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq n}

by similar reasoning. Since the matrix
(I-VV^{\top})
is idempotent, we have that

\eta_{i}=VV^{\top}\eta_{i}+(I-VV^{\top})\eta_{i}

where
VV^{\top}\eta_{i}
is independent from
(I-VV^{\top})\eta_{i}
and
V^{\top}\eta_{i}
is standard normally distributed, both conditionally and unconditionally on
V
. We can write

\eta_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\eta_{i}=VV^{\top}\eta_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}VV^{\top}\eta_{i}+G_{i},

where

G_{i}\equiv G_{i}(\eta_{i}):=(I-VV^{\top}){\eta}_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}(I-VV^{\top}){\eta}_{i}

and
G_{i}
independent of
VV^{\top}\eta_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}VV^{\top}\eta_{i}
. Let
\tilde{\eta}_{i}
be identically distributed to
\eta_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,n
. Combining the above, we have that

\displaystyle X^{(j)}_{L}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\left\{V\left(\frac{\|Z\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n}}+V^{\top}\eta_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}V^{\top}\eta_{i}\right)+G_{i}\right\}_{i\in[n]}=:({C}_{i})_{i\in[n]},

(S.47)

\displaystyle\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L}

\displaystyle\overset{d}{=}\left\{{V}\left(\frac{\left\|(I_{d}+\frac{nc_{\alpha}^{1/2}\rho^{2}}{d}\bar{\Gamma})^{1/2}Z\right\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n}}+{V}^{\top}\tilde{\eta}_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}V^{\top}\tilde{\eta}_{i}\right)+G_{i}\right\}_{i\in[n]}=:(\tilde{C}_{i})_{i\in[n]}.

(S.48)

As further notations, we introduce

\displaystyle\zeta_{i}

\displaystyle:={\|Z\|_{2}}/{\sqrt{n}}+V^{\top}\eta_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}V^{\top}{\eta}_{i}

\displaystyle\tilde{\zeta_{i}}

\displaystyle:={\|(I_{d_{L}}+\frac{nc_{\alpha}^{1/2}\rho^{2}}{d_{L}}\bar{\Gamma})^{1/2}Z\|_{2}}/{\sqrt{n}}+{V}^{\top}\tilde{\eta}_{i}-\frac{1}{n}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}V^{\top}\tilde{\eta}_{i}.

We have that
\zeta_{i}|Z\sim N\left(\frac{\|Z\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n}},\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\right)
and

\tilde{\zeta}_{i}|Z\sim N\left(\frac{\left\|(I_{d_{L}}+\frac{nc_{\alpha}^{1/2}\rho^{2}}{d}\bar{\Gamma})^{1/2}Z\right\|_{2}}{\sqrt{n}},\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)\right).

By e.g. Lemma
33
, we find that their respective push forward measures
{\mathbb{P}}^{\zeta_{i}|Z}
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{\tilde{\zeta}_{i}|Z}
satisfy

\displaystyle\|{\mathbb{P}}^{\zeta_{i}|Z}-{\mathbb{P}}^{\tilde{\zeta}_{i}|Z}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2(1-1/n)\sqrt{n}}\left|{\|Z\|_{2}}-{\left\|(I_{d_{L}}+\frac{nc_{\alpha}^{1/2}\rho^{2}}{d_{L}}\bar{\Gamma})^{1/2}Z\right\|_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}c_{\alpha}^{1/2}\rho^{2}}{d_{L}}\left|\frac{Z^{\top}\bar{\Gamma}Z}{\sqrt{Z^{\top}I_{d_{L}}Z}+\sqrt{Z^{\top}(I_{d_{L}}+\frac{nc_{\alpha}^{1/2}\rho^{2}}{d_{L}}\bar{\Gamma})Z}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\|\bar{\Gamma}\|\frac{\sqrt{n}c_{\alpha}^{1/2}\rho^{2}}{d_{L}}\|Z\|_{2},

where the second inequality follows from
n>1
in addition to the identity
(\sqrt{a}-\sqrt{b})(\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b})=a-b
and the final inequality follows from the fact that
\bar{\Gamma}
is positive semidefinite and
\bar{\Gamma}\leq\|\bar{\Gamma}\|I_{d_{L}}
. By Lemma
32
, there exists a coupling of
\zeta_{i}|Z
and
\tilde{\zeta}_{i}|Z
such that

{\mathbb{P}}\left(\zeta_{i}\neq\tilde{\zeta}_{i}|Z\right)\leq\frac{\sqrt{n}c_{\alpha}^{1/2}\rho^{2}\|\bar{\Gamma}\|\|Z\|_{2}}{2d_{L}}\wedge 1.

(S.49)

Take
{\mathbb{P}}^{\zeta_{i},\tilde{\zeta}_{i}|Z}
satisfying (
S.49
),
G_{i}=\tilde{G}_{i}
and set
(X^{(j)}_{L},\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{L})=(C,\tilde{C})
under
{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi,0}
. We obtain that
(\tilde{C}_{i},C_{i})
is independent of
(\tilde{C}_{k},C_{k})
for
k\neq i
and
\tilde{\zeta}_{i}=\zeta_{i}
implies
\tilde{C}_{i}=C_{i}
. By the independence structure, it holds for any joint distribution
{\mathbb{P}}
of
V
,
Z
,
\zeta=(\zeta_{i})_{i\in[n]}
,
\tilde{\zeta}_{1},\dots,\tilde{\zeta}_{n},(V,Z)
and
G=(G_{i})_{i\in[n]}
that

d{\mathbb{P}}^{C,\tilde{C}}=d\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\bigotimes}}{\mathbb{P}}^{C_{i},\tilde{C}_{i}}.

Consequently,

\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\text{1}\{\tilde{C}_{i}\neq{C}_{i}\}\sim\text{Bin}(n,p),

with
p:={\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{C}_{i}\neq{C}_{i}\right)
. By (
S.49
),
\|\bar{\Gamma}\|\asymp 1
and the fact that
\|Z\|_{2}
is
\sqrt{d}
-sub-exponential (using e.g. Proposition 2.7.1 in
[
83
]
), we obtain that

p={\mathbb{E}}^{Z}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\zeta_{i}\neq\tilde{\zeta}_{i}|Z\right)\leq\frac{\tilde{C}n^{1/2}(c_{\alpha}^{1/4}\rho)^{2}}{d^{1/2}_{L}}\wedge 1,

for a universal constant
\tilde{C}>0
. Since
[\zeta_{i}=\tilde{\zeta}_{i}]|Z
implies
[C_{i}=\tilde{C}_{i}]|Z
, we have that

\displaystyle p={\mathbb{P}}(C_{i}=\tilde{C_{i}})\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{Z}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\zeta_{i}\neq\tilde{\zeta}_{i}|Z\right)\leq\frac{\tilde{C}n^{1/2}c_{\alpha}^{1/4}\rho^{2}}{d^{1/2}_{L}}.

To summarize, we have now obtained that there exists a joint distribution
{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi,0}
of
{(X^{(j)},\tilde{X}^{(j)})}
such that
\left(X^{(j)},\tilde{X}^{(j)}\right)
satisfy

S:=\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\text{1}\{\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{i}\neq{X}^{(j)}_{i}\}\sim\text{Bin}(n,p),

and

p={\mathbb{P}}\left(X_{i}^{(j)}\neq\tilde{X}_{i}^{(j)}\right)\leq\frac{\tilde{C}n^{1/2}(c_{\alpha}^{1/4}\rho)^{2}}{d^{1/2}_{L}}\wedge 1.

Let
{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi,0}
denote its corresponding expectation. Consequently, by applying Lemma
16
, we have for any measurable
A
that

\displaystyle P_{\pi}^{n}K^{j}(A|\tilde{X}^{(j)})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\pi,0}K^{j}(A|\tilde{X}^{(j)})={\mathbb{E}}^{\tilde{X}^{(j)},X^{(j)}}_{\pi,0}K^{j}(A|\tilde{X}^{(j)})

\displaystyle\leq e^{4\epsilon np}P_{0}K^{j}(A|{X}^{(j)})+2\delta npe^{2\epsilon np}.

It follows that

\displaystyle e^{4\epsilon np}

\displaystyle\leq 1+C^{\prime}\frac{\epsilon n^{3/2}\rho^{2}}{\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}d^{1/2}_{L}},

for a universal constant
C^{\prime}>0
, where the inequality follows from the fact that under the assumptions on
\rho^{2}
(i.e. condition (
S.8
) or (
S.9
)) that

\frac{\epsilon n^{3/2}c_{\alpha}^{1/2}\rho^{2}}{d^{1/2}_{L}}\leq\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}/\sqrt{m}

and a sufficiently small enough choice of
c_{\alpha}>0
. Similarly, using that
\delta\leq\epsilon/\sqrt{m}
,

\displaystyle\delta npe^{2\epsilon np}

\displaystyle\leq\delta+C^{\prime}c_{\alpha}/m^{3/2}.

Case 2:
Consider
\epsilon\leq 1/\sqrt{mnd_{L}}
. We will make use of the total variation coupling between
\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{i}\sim N(f,I_{d_{L}})
and
X^{(j)}_{i}\sim N(0,I_{d_{L}})
, as given by Lemma
32
. Since

\|N(0,I_{d_{L}})-N(f,I_{d_{L}})\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}\|f\|_{2}\right)\wedge 2

(see e.g. Lemma
33
), we can couple the two data sets observation wise independently (simply taking the product space) such that

\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\text{1}\{\tilde{X}^{(j)}_{i}\neq X^{(j)}_{i}\}|f\sim\text{Bin}(n,p_{f})

where
p_{f}=(\|f\|_{2}/4)\wedge 1
. Given
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\|f\|_{2}\overset{d}{=}d^{-1/2}_{L}c_{\alpha}^{1/4}\rho\|N(0,I_{d_{L}})\|_{2}
and
\|N(0,I_{d_{L}})\|_{2}
is
\sqrt{d_{L}}
-sub-exponential we obtain (using e.g. Proposition 2.7.1 in
[
83
]
)

\displaystyle\int p^{k}_{f}d\pi(f)\leq\int(\|f\|_{2}/4)^{k}d\pi(f)\leq\tilde{C}^{k}k^{k}(c_{\alpha}^{1/4}\rho)^{k},

for a universal constant
\tilde{C}>0
. The assumed condition on
\rho
yields

{\epsilon nc_{\alpha}^{1/4}\rho}\leq c_{\alpha}^{1/4}/\sqrt{m},

which by similar arguments as before implies

\displaystyle e^{4\epsilon np}

\displaystyle\leq 1+C^{\prime}c_{\alpha}^{1/4}/\sqrt{m},

\displaystyle\delta npe^{2\epsilon np}

\displaystyle\leq\delta+C^{\prime}c_{\alpha}^{1/2}/m^{3/2},

for a universal constant
C^{\prime}>0
. By applying the claim at the start of the lemma and using the assumptions on
\rho
, we obtain that

\displaystyle P_{\pi}^{n}K^{j}(A|\tilde{X}^{(j)})

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\pi,0}K^{j}(A|\tilde{X}^{(j)})=\int{\mathbb{E}}_{f,0}K^{j}(A|\tilde{X}^{(j)})d\pi(f)

\displaystyle\leq(1+Cc_{\alpha}^{1/4}/\sqrt{m})P_{0}K^{j}(A|{X}^{(j)})+\delta+Cc_{\alpha}^{1/2}/m^{3/2}

as desired. Again, (
S.44
) follows by similar steps.
∎

With the above two lemmas in hand, we are ready to prove Lemma
15
.

Write
{\mathcal{L}}_{\pi,u}^{j}(Y^{(j)})\equiv{\mathcal{L}}_{\pi}^{j}
and let
V_{\pi}\equiv V_{\pi}^{j}:={\mathcal{L}}_{\pi}^{j}-1
. Using that
{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(\tilde{X}^{(j)})=1
and that by the law of total probability

{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[\mathscr{L}_{\pi}(\tilde{X}^{(j)})\bigg|Y^{(j)},U=u\right]=1,

it follows that
{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}=0
and

{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}\left({\mathcal{L}}_{\pi}^{j}\right)^{2}=1+{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}{\mathcal{L}}_{\pi}^{j}({\mathcal{L}}_{\pi}^{j}-1)=1+{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}.

Define
V_{\pi}^{+}:=0\vee V_{\pi}
and let
V_{\pi}^{-}=-(0\wedge V_{\pi})
, which are both nonnegative random variables, with
V_{\pi}=V_{\pi}^{+}-V_{\pi}^{-}
. We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}^{+}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}\left(V_{\pi}^{+}\geq t\right)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}\left(V_{\pi}^{+}\geq t\right)dt+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}\left(V_{\pi}^{+}\geq t\right)dt.

(S.50)

Taking
T=\frac{5m^{1/2}}{{\alpha}}
, the second term is equal to zero as

V_{\pi}^{+}\leq|{{\mathcal{L}}}_{\pi,u}^{j}(y)-1|\leq\frac{5m^{1/2}}{{\alpha}}\;\;\;P_{0}K^{j}(\cdot|X^{(j)},u)\text{-a.s.}

and
P_{\pi}\sim P_{0}
(which in turn implies
{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}\sim{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}
). The integrand of the first term equals
P_{\pi}^{n}K^{j}(\{V_{\pi}\geq t\}|X^{(j)},u)
. By Lemma
17
, it holds that

P_{\pi}K^{j}(\{V_{\pi}\geq t\}|X^{(j)},u)\leq\left(1+c_{\alpha}^{1/4}m^{-1/2}\right)P_{0}K^{j}(\{V_{\pi}\geq t\}|X^{(j)},u)+\delta+\frac{c_{\alpha}}{m^{3/2}}.

It follows that (
S.50
) is bounded from above by

\displaystyle\left(1+c_{\alpha}^{1/4}m^{-1/2}\right)\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}\left(V_{\pi}^{+}\geq t\right)dt+T\delta+T\frac{c_{\alpha}}{m^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(1+c_{\alpha}^{1/4}m^{-1/2}\right){\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}^{+}+T\delta+T\frac{c_{\alpha}}{m^{3/2}}.

Similarly, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}^{-}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}\left(V_{\pi}^{-}\geq t\right)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}\left(V_{\pi}^{-}\geq t\right)dt+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}\left(V_{\pi}^{-}\geq t\right)dt.

(S.51)

Choosing
T\geq 1
here results in the second term being zero, as
L_{\pi}^{j}\geq 0
. Applying Lemma
17
, the right-hand side of the above display is further bounded from below by

\displaystyle\left(1-c_{\alpha}^{1/4}m^{-1/2}\right)\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}\left(V_{\pi}^{-}\geq t\right)dt-T\delta-T\frac{c_{\alpha}}{m^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\left(1-c_{\alpha}^{1/4}m^{-1/2}\right){\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}^{-}-T\delta-T\frac{c_{\alpha}}{m^{3/2}},

where the inequality uses
V_{\pi}^{-}\leq 1
.

Combining the above bounds with the fact that
V_{\pi}^{+}+V_{\pi}^{-}=|V_{\pi}|
and
{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}=0
yields that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}^{+}-{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}^{-}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{c_{\alpha}^{1/4}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}|V_{\pi}|}{\sqrt{m}}+2T\delta+2T\frac{c_{\alpha}}{m^{3/2}}.

Plugging in the choice of
T=5m^{1/2}/\alpha
and using that
\delta\leq c_{\alpha}m^{-3/2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}\leq\frac{c_{\alpha}^{1/4}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}|V_{\pi}|}{\sqrt{m}}+\frac{20c_{\alpha}}{m\alpha}.

If
{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}|V_{\pi}|\lesssim m^{-1/2}
, we obtain
{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}\lesssim m^{-1}(c_{\alpha}^{1/4}+c_{\alpha}/\alpha)
. Assume next that
{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}|V_{\pi}|\gtrsim m^{-1/2}
. Then,

{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}\lesssim\frac{c_{\alpha}^{1/4}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}|V_{\pi}|}{\sqrt{m}}.

Since
{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}={\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}^{2}
and using that by Cauchy–Schwarz
{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}|V_{\pi}|
is bounded above by
\sqrt{{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}^{2}}
, we obtain that

\sqrt{{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}^{2}}\lesssim C^{\prime}c_{\alpha}^{1/4}m^{-1/2}

for a universal constant
C^{\prime}>0
depending only on
\alpha
. In both cases, we obtain that

\mathrm{B}^{\pi}_{u}=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}\left(1+{\mathbb{E}}_{\pi}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}\right)=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}\left(1+{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}V_{\pi}^{2}\right)\leq e^{C\sqrt{c_{\alpha}}}

for universal constant
C>0
, finishing the proof of the lemma.
∎

A.5.2
Proof of Lemma
13

Consider without loss of generality
\sigma=1
(the general result follows by the
\sigma^{-1}
rescaling). The bound
\text{Tr}(\Xi_{u}^{j})\leq nd_{L}
follows by the fact that conditional expectation contracts the
L_{2}
-norm; let
v\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}}
, then

\displaystyle v^{\top}\Xi_{u}^{j}v

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}\left[v^{\top}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}{X}^{(j)}_{i}\bigg|Y^{(j)},U=u\right]{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}\left[\left(\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}{X}^{(j)}_{i}\right)^{\top}v\bigg|Y^{(j)},U=u\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y^{(j)}}{\mathbb{E}}_{0}^{Y|U=u}\left[v^{\top}\left(\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}{X}^{(j)}_{i}\right)\bigg|Y^{(j)},U=u\right]^{2}.

Since the conditional expectation contracts the
L_{2}
-norm, we obtain that the latter is bounded by

{\mathbb{E}}_{0}v^{\top}\left(\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}{X}^{(j)}_{i}\right)\left(\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}{X}^{(j)}_{i}\right)^{\top}v=n\|v\|_{2}^{2},

which completes the proof of the statement “
\Xi_{u}^{j}\leq nI_{d_{L}}
” and “
\text{Tr}(\Xi_{u}^{j})\leq nd_{L}
”.

For second other bound on the trace, we start introducing the notations
\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X^{(j)}_{L;i}
and

G_{i}=\left\langle{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[n\overline{X^{(j)}}_{L}|Y^{(j)},U=u\right],X^{(j)}_{L;i}\right\rangle.

For the remainder of the proof, consider versions of
X^{(j)}_{L}
and
Y^{(j)}
defined on the same probability given
U=u
, and we shall write as a shorthand

{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\equiv{\mathbb{P}}^{(X^{(j)},Y^{(j)})|U=u}_{0}\text{ and }{\mathbb{E}}^{j}\equiv{\mathbb{E}}^{(X^{(j)},Y^{(j)})|U=u}_{0}.

Consider random variables
V,W
defined on the same probability space. It holds that

{\mathbb{E}}W{\mathbb{E}}[W|V]={\mathbb{E}}{\mathbb{E}}[W|V]{\mathbb{E}}[W|V],

since
W-{\mathbb{E}}[W|V]
is orthogonal to
{\mathbb{E}}[W|V]
. Combining this fact with the linearity of the inner product and conditional expectation, we see that

\text{Tr}(\Xi_{u}^{j})={\mathbb{E}}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}_{0}\left\|{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[n\overline{X^{(j)}}|Y^{(j)},U=u]\right\|_{2}^{2}=\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}{\mathbb{E}}^{j}G_{i}.

(S.52)

Define also

\breve{G}_{i}=\left\langle{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[n\overline{X^{(j)}}|Y^{(j)},U=u],\breve{X}_{i}^{(j)}\right\rangle,

where
\breve{X}_{i}^{(j)}
is an independent copy of
{X}_{i}^{(j)}
(defined on the same, possibly enlarged probability space) and note that
{\mathbb{E}}^{j}\breve{G}_{i}=0
. Write
G_{i}^{+}:=0\vee G_{i}
and
G_{i}^{-}=-(0\wedge G_{i})
. We have

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{j}G_{i}^{+}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt=\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt+T\delta+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt+2\epsilon\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt+T\delta+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}_{0}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt+2\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt+T\delta+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{+}\geq t\right),

where in the second to last inequality follows by Taylor expansion and the fact that
\epsilon\leq 1
. Similarly, we obtain

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{j}G_{i}^{-}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)

\displaystyle\geq e^{-\epsilon}\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt-T\delta

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt-2\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt-T\delta

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt-2\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt-T\delta-\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt.

Putting these together with
G_{i}=G_{i}^{+}-G_{i}^{-}
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{j}G_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt-\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt+2\epsilon\int_{0}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(|\breve{G}_{i}|\geq t\right)dt

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;+2T\delta+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{j}\breve{G}_{i}+2\epsilon{\mathbb{E}}^{j}|\breve{G}_{i}|+2T\delta+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt.

The first term in the last display equals
0
. For the second term, observe that

\breve{G}_{i}\bigg|\left[Y^{(j)},X^{(j)},U=u\right]\sim N(0,\|{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[n\overline{X^{(j)}}|Y^{(j)},U=u]\|_{2}^{2}),

so

{\mathbb{E}}^{j}|\breve{G}_{i}|={\mathbb{E}}^{X^{(j)},Y^{(j)}}{\mathbb{E}}^{\breve{X}^{(j)}}|\breve{G}_{i}|={\mathbb{E}}\|{\mathbb{E}}[n\overline{X^{(j)}}|Y^{(j)},U=u]\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{\text{Tr}(\Xi_{u}^{j})}
