where the last inequality is Cauchy–Schwarz. To bound the terms

\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt+\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt

we shall employ tail bounds, which follow after showing that
G_{i}
is
\sqrt{d_{L}n}
-sub-exponential. To see this, note that by Jensen’s inequality followed by the law of total probability,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{j}e^{t|G_{i}|}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{j}e^{t|\langle{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[n\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}|Y^{(j)},U=u],X_{L;i}^{(j)}\rangle|}

\displaystyle=\int\int e^{t|\langle{\mathbb{E}}_{0}[n\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}|Y^{(j)}=y,U=u],x\rangle|}d{\mathbb{P}}^{X^{(j)}|Y^{(j)}=y,U=u}_{0}(x)d{\mathbb{P}}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}_{0}(y)

\displaystyle\leq\int\int{\mathbb{E}}_{0}\left[e^{t|\langle n\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}},x\rangle|}|Y^{(j)}=y,U=u\right]d{\mathbb{P}}^{X^{(j)}|Y^{(j)}=y,U=u}_{0}(x)d{\mathbb{P}}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}_{0}(y)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{X^{(j)}}_{0}e^{t|\langle n\overline{X^{(j)}}_{L},{X}_{L;i}^{(j)}\rangle|},

where the last equality follows from the fact that conditional expectation contracts the
L_{1}
-norm and the fact that
U
is independent of
X^{(j)}
. Next, we bound
{\mathbb{E}}^{X^{(j)}}_{0}e^{t|\langle n\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}},X_{L;i}^{(j)}\rangle|}
.

By the triangle inequality and independence,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{X^{(j)}}_{0}e^{t|\langle n\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}},{X}_{L;i}^{(j)}\rangle|}

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{E}}^{X^{(j)}}_{0}e^{t|\langle\sum_{k\neq i}^{n}X^{(j)}_{L;k},{X}_{L;i}^{(j)}\rangle|}{\mathbb{E}}^{X^{(j)}_{i}}_{0}e^{t|\langle{X^{(j)}_{L;i}},{X}_{L;i}^{(j)}\rangle|}.

The random variable
\langle{X^{(j)}_{L;i}},{X}_{L;i}^{(j)}\rangle
is
\chi_{d_{L}}^{2}
-distributed, so by standard computations (see e.g. Lemma 12 in
[
76
]
) we obtain that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{X^{(j)}_{i}}_{0}e^{t|\langle{X^{(j)}_{L;i}},{X}_{L;i}^{(j)}\rangle|}=\left({\mathbb{E}}e^{tN(0,1)^{2}\rangle|}\right)^{d_{L}}\leq e^{td_{L}+2t^{2}d_{L}},

whenever
t\leq 1/4
. We also find that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{X^{(j)}}_{0}e^{t|\langle\sum_{k\neq i}^{n}X^{(j)}_{L;k},{X}_{L;i}^{(j)}\rangle|}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{(X^{(j)}_{k})_{k\neq i}}_{0}{\mathbb{E}}^{X^{(j)}_{i}}_{0}e^{t|\langle\sum_{k\neq i}^{n}X^{(j)}_{L;k},{X}_{L;i}^{(j)}\rangle|}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{X^{(j)}}e^{\frac{t^{2}}{2}\left\|\sum_{k\neq i}^{n}X^{(j)}_{L;k}\right\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\left({\mathbb{E}}e^{\frac{t^{2}(n-1)}{2}N(0,1)^{2}}\right)^{d_{L}}

\displaystyle\leq e^{\frac{1}{2}({t^{2}(n-1)}d_{L}+t^{4}(n-1)^{2}d_{L})},

where the inequality follows again by e.g. Lemma 12 in
[
76
]
if
{t^{2}(n-1)^{2}}\leq 1/2
. By the fact that
G_{i}^{+}\leq|G_{i}|
and Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}^{j}(G_{i}^{+}>T)\leq{\mathbb{P}}^{j}(|G_{i}|>T)\leq e^{-tT}{\mathbb{E}}^{j}e^{t|G_{i}|},\text{ for all }T,t>0.

Combined with the above bounds for the moment generating function means that for
\delta=0
, the result follows from letting
T\to\infty
. If
\delta>0
, take
T=8(d+L\vee\sqrt{nd_{L}})\log(1/\delta)
to obtain that

\displaystyle\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}\left(G_{i}^{+}\geq t\right)dt\leq e^{-\log(1/\delta)}.

It is easy to see that the same bound applies to
\int_{T}^{\infty}{\mathbb{P}}^{j}_{0}\left(\breve{G}_{i}^{-}\geq t\right)dt
. We obtain that

\displaystyle\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}{\mathbb{E}}^{j}G_{i}

\displaystyle\leq 2n\epsilon\sqrt{\text{Tr}(\Xi_{u}^{j})}+16\delta(d_{L}\vee\sqrt{nd_{L}})\log(1/\delta)+2n\delta.

If
\sqrt{\text{Tr}(\Xi_{u}^{j})}\leq n\epsilon
, the lemma holds (there is nothing to prove). So assume instead that
\sqrt{\text{Tr}(\Xi_{u}^{j})}\geq n\epsilon
. Combining the above display with (
S.52
), we get

\displaystyle\sqrt{\text{Tr}(\Xi_{u}^{j})}

\displaystyle\leq{2n\epsilon}+16\delta\frac{d_{L}\vee\sqrt{nd_{L}}}{n\epsilon}\log(1/\delta)+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\delta.

Since
x^{p}\log(1/x)
tends to
0
as
x\to 0
for any
p>0
, the result follows for
\delta\leq\left(\left(\frac{n}{d_{L}}\wedge\frac{n^{1/2}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\right)\epsilon^{2}\right)^{1+p}
for some
p>0
as this implies that the last two terms are
O(n\epsilon)
.
∎

B
Proofs related to the optimal testing strategies

The proofs concerning the three sections
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
are given in this section, divided across the subsections
B.1
,
B.2
and
B.3
respectively.

We recall the notations
d_{L}=\sum_{l=1}^{L}2^{l}
for
L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
f_{L}=\Pi_{L}f
for
f\in\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})
and
\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X^{(j)}_{L;i}
for
j=1,\dots,m
.

B.1
Procedure I

We briefly recall the testing procedure outlined in Section
3.1
. Let
\tau>0
,
L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
d_{L}:=\sum^{L}_{l=1}2^{l}
and
V^{(j)}_{L;\tau}\sim\chi^{2}_{d_{L}}
independent of
X^{(j)}
the random map from
({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}
to
{\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\tilde{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}(x)=\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\left(\left\|\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{n}\overline{x}\right\|^{2}_{2}-{V^{(j)}_{L;\tau}}\right)\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}.

(S.53)

Define furthermore

D_{\tau}=\frac{\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\log(N)\left(\sqrt{n\sqrt{d_{L}}\tau}\vee\sqrt{nd_{L}}\right)}{n\sqrt{d_{L}}}.

(S.54)

Set
K_{\tau}=\lceil 2\tau D^{-1}_{\tau}\rceil
and consider the set
{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}={\mathcal{A}}_{L;\tau}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{L;\tau}
, where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{A}}_{L;\tau}

\displaystyle=\left\{(x_{i})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\infty})^{n}:\bigg|\|\sigma^{-1}\textstyle\sum_{i\in{\mathcal{J}}}\Pi_{L}x_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-kd_{L}\bigg|\leq\frac{1}{8}kD_{\tau}n\sqrt{d_{L}}\;\;\forall{\mathcal{J}}\subset[n],|{\mathcal{J}}|=k\leq K_{\tau}\right\},

(S.55)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{L;\tau}

\displaystyle=\left\{(x_{i})\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\infty})^{n}:\left|\langle\sigma^{-1}\Pi_{L}x_{i},\sigma^{-1}\textstyle\sum_{k\neq i}\Pi_{L}x_{k}\rangle\right|\leq\frac{1}{8}kD_{\tau}n\sqrt{d_{L}},\;\;\forall i=1,\dots,n\right\}.

Lemma
18
below shows that
X^{(j)}
concentrates on
{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}
under the null hypothesis.

Lemma 18
.

Whenever
\sigma^{-2}n\|\Pi_{L}f\|_{2}^{2}d^{-1/2}_{L}\leq\tau/2
,
\tau\leq nR^{2}/\sqrt{d_{L}}
and
\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}
is taken large enough, it holds that

{\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(X^{(j)}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}\right)\leq\frac{\alpha}{2N}.

Since
{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}={\mathcal{A}}_{L;\tau}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{L;\tau}
, it suffices to show that
{\mathcal{A}}_{L;\tau}^{c}
and
{\mathcal{B}}_{L;\tau}^{c}
as defined in (
S.55
) are small in
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}
-probability for a large enough choice of
\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}>0
.

Define
\eta_{\tau}:=D_{\tau}n\sqrt{d_{L}}/8
. For both sets, we proceed via a union bound:

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(X^{(j)}\notin{\mathcal{A}}_{L;\tau}\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(\exists{\mathcal{J}}\subset[n],\,|{\mathcal{J}}|\leq K_{\tau}:\left|\|\textstyle\underset{i\in{\mathcal{J}}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\sigma^{-1}X_{L;i}^{(j)}\|_{2}^{2}-|{\mathcal{J}}|d_{L}\right|>|{\mathcal{J}}|\eta_{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\underset{k=1}{\overset{K_{\tau}}{\sum}}{n\choose k}\text{Pr}\left(\left|\|\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{k}f_{L}-Z\|_{2}^{2}-d_{L}\right|>\eta_{\tau}\right)

(S.56)

where we recall that
f_{L}
is the projection of
f
onto its first
d_{L}
coordinates and
Z\sim N(0,I_{d_{L}})
. We have

\|\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{k}f_{L}-Z\|_{2}^{2}=\sigma^{-2}k\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}-2\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{k}f_{L}^{\top}Z+\|Z\|_{2}^{2}.

Recalling that
K_{\tau}=\lceil 2\tau D^{-1}_{\tau}\rceil
, we obtain that

K_{\tau}\leq\frac{2\tau n\sqrt{d_{L}}}{\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\log(N)(\sqrt{n\sqrt{d_{L}}\tau}\vee\sqrt{nd_{L}})}\leq\frac{\sqrt{n\sqrt{d_{L}}\tau}}{\tilde{\kappa_{\alpha}}\log(N)}\lesssim\frac{\eta_{\tau}}{\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\log(N)}.

(S.57)

By the assumptions of the lemma (
\sigma^{-2}n\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\tau\sqrt{d_{L}}/2
and
\tau\leq nR^{2}/\sqrt{d_{L}}
), we obtain that
\sigma^{-2}k\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\leq K_{\tau}R^{2}
. Consequently, we have that for
\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}>0
large enough
\sigma^{-2}K_{\tau}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}<\eta_{\tau}/2
, so it holds that

\text{Pr}\left(\|\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{k}f-Z\|_{2}^{2}-d_{L}>\eta_{\tau}\right)\leq\text{Pr}\left(\|Z\|_{2}^{2}-d_{L}-2\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{k}f^{\top}_{L}Z>\eta_{\tau}/2\right).

Using that
\text{Pr}(A\cap B)+\text{Pr}(A\cap B^{c})\leq\text{Pr}(A^{\prime})+\text{Pr}(A\cap B^{c})
for
A\cap B\subset A^{\prime}
, it follows that the latter display is bounded above by

\text{Pr}\left(\|Z\|_{2}^{2}-d_{L}>\eta_{\tau}/4\right)+\text{Pr}\left(-2\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{k}f^{\top}_{L}Z>\eta_{\tau}/4\right).

By e.g. Lemma
36
, the first probability is bounded by
e^{-d\eta_{\tau}/8}
. Again using
K_{\tau}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}<\eta_{\tau}/2
, the second term is bounded by
e^{-\eta_{\tau}/32}
, where we note that the second term equals zero in the case that
f=0
. The bound

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left(\|\sigma^{-1}\sqrt{k}f_{L}-Z\|_{2}^{2}-d_{L}<-\eta_{\tau}\right)\leq e^{-d_{L}\eta_{\tau}/4}+e^{-\eta_{\tau}/8}

follows by similar reasoning. Combining the above with the elementary bound
\sum^{K_{\tau}}_{k=1}{n\choose k}\leq e^{K_{\tau}\log(n)}
and (
S.57
) means that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(X^{(j)}\notin{\mathcal{A}}_{L;\tau}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\exp\left(K_{\tau}\log(N)-\frac{(1+d_{L}/2)\eta_{\tau}}{4}\right)\leq\alpha/(4mn).

Turning our attention to
{\mathcal{B}}_{L;\tau}
, we find that
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(X^{(j)}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{L;\tau}\right)
is equal to

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(\underset{i\in[n]}{\overset{}{\max}}\sigma^{-2}\left|\underset{k\in[n]\setminus\{i\}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\langle X_{i}^{(j)},X_{k}^{(j)}\rangle\right|>\eta_{\tau}\right)\leq n\text{Pr}\bigg(\left|\langle\sigma^{-1}f+Z,(n-1)\sigma^{-1}f+\sqrt{n-1}Z^{\prime}\rangle\right|>

\displaystyle\eta_{\tau}\bigg),

where
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are independent
N(0,I_{d})
random vectors. Using another union bound, the above is further bounded by

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\bigg(

\displaystyle\sqrt{n-1}\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle>\eta_{\tau}/2-(n-1)\|\sigma^{-1}f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\bigg)+\text{Pr}\bigg(({n-1})\langle\sigma^{-1}f,Z^{\prime}\rangle+\sqrt{n-1}\langle\sigma^{-1}f,Z\rangle>\eta_{\tau}/2\bigg).

(S.58)

Using that
n\|\sigma^{-1}f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\tau\sqrt{d_{L}}/2
and
\tau\leq nR^{2}/\sqrt{d_{L}}
by assumption of the lemma, we see that

n\|\sigma^{-1}f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\tau\sqrt{d_{L}}/2\leq\sqrt{n\sqrt{d}\tau}\frac{\sqrt{\sqrt{d}\tau}}{2\sqrt{n}}\leq\frac{R}{\log(N)\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}}\eta_{\tau}.

(S.59)

For
\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}>0
, the latter can be seen to be larger than
\eta_{\tau}/4
. Consequently, the first term in (
S.58
) can be seen to be bounded by

\text{Pr}\bigg({\sqrt{n-1}}\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle>\eta_{\tau}/4\bigg)\leq e^{-\frac{\eta_{\tau}}{4\sqrt{nd_{L}}}}\leq e^{-\kappa_{\alpha}\log(N)},

where the inequality follows from Lemma
37
. Since
Z
and
Z^{\prime}
are independent standard Gaussian vectors, the second term in (
S.58
) is bounded above by

\text{Pr}\bigg({\sqrt{2}({n-1})}\langle\sigma^{-1}f,Z\rangle>\eta_{\tau}/2\bigg)\leq e^{-\frac{\eta_{\tau}^{2}}{8n^{2}\|\sigma^{-2}f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}}.

By
n\|\sigma^{-1}f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\tau\sqrt{d_{L}}/2
,

n^{2}\|\sigma^{-1}f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\leq n\tau\sqrt{d_{L}}/2\leq\frac{\eta_{\tau}^{2}}{\log^{2}(N)\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}^{2}}.

Hence, we have obtained that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(X^{(j)}\notin{\mathcal{B}}_{L;\tau}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{4mn}

for
\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}>0
large enough. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

In Lemma
19
, it is shown that
x\mapsto S^{(j)}(x)
is
D_{\tau}
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hamming distance on
{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}
, with
D_{\tau}
as defined in (
S.54
).

Lemma 19
.

The map
x\mapsto S^{(j)}_{\tau}(x)
defined in (
14
) is
D_{\tau}
-Lipschitz with respect to
({\mathbb{R}}^{d})^{n}
-Hamming distance on
{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}
.

Consider
x=(x_{i})_{i\in[n]},\breve{x}=(\breve{x})_{i\in[n]}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}
with
k:=\mathrm{d}_{H}(x,\breve{x})
. If
k>\lceil 2\tau D^{-1}_{\tau}\rceil
, we have
|S^{(j)}_{\tau}(x)-S^{(j)}_{\tau}(\breve{x})|\leq 2\tau\leq D_{\tau}k
. If
k\leq\lceil 2\tau D^{-1}_{\tau}\rceil
, let
{\mathcal{J}}\subset[n]
denote the indexes of columns in which
x
and
\breve{x}
differ. Define the sum of the elements that
x
and
\breve{x}
have in common as

v=\sigma^{-1}\sum_{i\in[n]\setminus{\mathcal{J}}}x_{i},\text{ such that }\sigma^{-1}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}x_{i}=v+w\text{ and }\sigma^{-1}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\breve{x}_{i}=v+\breve{w}.

We have

\displaystyle S^{(j)}_{\tau}(x)-S^{(j)}_{\tau}(\breve{x})

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\left(\left\|n^{-1}(v+w)\right\|^{2}-\frac{V^{(j)}}{n}\right)-\frac{n}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\left(\left\|n^{-1}(v+\breve{w})\right\|^{2}-\frac{V^{(j)}}{n}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n\sqrt{d_{L}}}\left(2\left\langle w,v\right\rangle-2\left\langle\breve{w},v\right\rangle+\left\|w\right\|_{2}^{2}-\left\|\breve{w}\right\|_{2}^{2}\right).

The last two terms are bounded by
kD_{\tau}/4
since
x,\breve{x}\in{\mathcal{A}}_{L;\tau}
. The first two terms equal

\displaystyle\frac{2}{n\sqrt{d_{L}}}\left(\left\langle w,v+w\right\rangle-\left\langle\breve{w},v+\breve{w}\right\rangle+\left\|\breve{w}\right\|_{2}^{2}-\left\|w\right\|_{2}^{2}\right),

where the last two terms are bounded by
kD_{\tau}/2
. It holds that

\displaystyle\left\langle w,v+w\right\rangle-\left\langle\breve{w},v+\breve{w}\right\rangle=\sigma^{-2}\underset{i\in{\mathcal{J}}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left(\left\langle x_{i},\underset{i\in[n]\setminus{\mathcal{J}}}{\overset{}{\sum}}x_{i}\right\rangle-\left\langle\breve{x}_{i},\underset{i\in[n]\setminus{\mathcal{J}}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\breve{x}_{i}\right\rangle+\|x_{i}\|_{2}^{2}-\|\breve{x}_{i}\|_{2}^{2}\right),

which is bounded by
kD_{\tau}/4
for
x\in{\mathcal{A}}_{L;\tau}\cap{\mathcal{B}}_{L;\tau}
. Putting it all together and by symmetry of the argument, we obtain that

\left|S^{(j)}_{\tau}(x)-S^{(j)}_{\tau}(\breve{x})\right|\leq D_{\tau}k.

∎

Lemma
20
below show that there exists a measurable function
{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
,
D_{\tau}
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hamming distance, such that
{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}(X^{(j)}_{L})=\tilde{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}(X^{(j)}_{L})
whenever
X^{(j)}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}
. That is, letting
\Psi_{L}:{\mathbb{R}}^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}}
be the coordinate projection for the first
d_{L}
coordinates, the lemma allows a Lipschitz extension of the test statistic defined in (
S.53
) on
\Psi_{L}{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}
to all of
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}}
. The proof follows essentially the construction of McShane
[
64
]
for obtaining a Lipschitz extension with respect to the Hamming distance, but our lemma verifies in addition the Borel-measurability of the resulting map.

We follow a construction that is in essence that of McShane
[
64
]
, whilst also verifying that such an extension is Borel measurable.

Lemma 20
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}\subset({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}
and
S:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be a (Borel) measurable
D
-Lipschitz map with respect to the Hamming distance on
({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}
. Then, there exists a map
\tilde{S}:({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
measurable with respect to the Borel sigma algebra such that it is
D
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hamming distance on
({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}
and
\tilde{S}=S
on
{\mathcal{C}}
.

The case where
{\mathcal{C}}
is empty is trivial, so we shall assume
{\mathcal{C}}
to be nonempty. Consider the map
\breve{S}:({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}\to[-\infty,\infty)
given by

\breve{S}(x)=\begin{cases}S(x)\;\text{ if }x\in{\mathcal{C}},\\
\inf\left\{S(c)+D\mathrm{d}_{H}(c,x)\,:\,c\in{\mathcal{C}}\right\}\;\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Fix any
c^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{C}}
. Since
S
is
D
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hamming distance, we have for all
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
that

S(c)+D\mathrm{d}_{H}(c,x)\geq S(c^{\prime})-D\mathrm{d}_{H}(c^{\prime},c)+D\mathrm{d}_{H}(c,x)\geq S(c^{\prime})-D\mathrm{d}_{H}(c^{\prime},x)>-\infty

where the last step follows from the triangle inequality. So,
\breve{S}
is real valued. For all
x\in({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}
and
\gamma>0
, there exists
c_{\gamma}\in{\mathcal{C}}
such that

\breve{S}(x)\geq S(c_{\gamma})+D\mathrm{d}_{H}(c_{\gamma},x)-\gamma.

So for
x,x^{\prime}\in\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})^{n}
,

\breve{S}(x^{\prime})-\breve{S}(x)\leq\breve{S}(c_{\gamma})+D\mathrm{d}_{H}(c_{\gamma},x^{\prime})-\breve{S}(c_{\gamma})-D\mathrm{d}_{H}(c_{\gamma},x)+\gamma\leq D\mathrm{d}_{H}(x,x^{\prime})+\gamma.

By symmetry of the argument and since
\gamma>0
is given arbitrarily, we conclude that
\breve{S}
is
D
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hamming distance. Note however, that this construction does not guarantee that
\breve{S}
is measurable.

For any map
H:({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}\to[-\infty,\infty]
, let
{H}^{*}
denote its minimal Borel-measurable majorant. That is, a measurable map
{H}^{*}:({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}\to[-\infty,\infty]
such that

1.

H\leq H^{*}
and

2.

H^{*}\leq T

{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
-a.s. for every measurable
T:({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}\to[-\infty,\infty]
with
T\geq H
.

Such a map exists by e.g. Lemma 1.2.1 in
[
82
]
. The map
\tilde{S}:({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\tilde{S}(x)=\breve{S}^{*}(x)\text{1}_{x\notin{\mathcal{C}}}+S(x)\text{1}_{x\in{\mathcal{C}}}

is measurable and can be seen to be a Borel-measurable majorant of
\breve{S}
; following from the fact that sums and products of measurable functions are measurable,
\breve{S}\leq\breve{S}^{*}
and
\breve{S}(x)=S(x)
for
x\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

Furthermore, by combining the fact that
\tilde{S}
is measurable with e.g. Lemma 1.2.2 in
[
82
]
, we get

\displaystyle|\tilde{S}(x)-\tilde{S}(x^{\prime})|=|(\tilde{S}(x)-\tilde{S}(x^{\prime}))^{*}|\leq|\breve{S}(x)-\breve{S}(x^{\prime})|^{*},

(S.60)

where
(x,x^{\prime})\mapsto|\breve{S}(x)-\breve{S}(x^{\prime})|^{*}
is minimal Borel-measurable majorant of
(x,x^{\prime})\mapsto|\breve{S}(x)-\breve{S}(x^{\prime})|
. Since
\breve{S}
is
D
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hamming distance
(x,x^{\prime})\mapsto\mathrm{d}_{H}(x,x^{\prime})
, which is a measurable map,

|\breve{S}(x)-\breve{S}(x^{\prime})|^{*}\leq D\mathrm{d}_{H}(x,x^{\prime}).

From (
S.60
) it follows that for all
x,x^{\prime}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}})^{n}
,

\displaystyle|\tilde{S}(x)-\tilde{S}(x^{\prime})|\leq D\mathrm{d}_{H}(x,x^{\prime}).

We have obtained a map
\tilde{S}
that is
D
-Lipschitz with respect to the Hamming distance, measurable and
\tilde{S}=S
on
{\mathcal{C}}
, concluding the proof.
∎

B.1.1
Proof of Lemma
1

Consider the transcript of Section
3.1
, which we recall here as

Y^{(j)}_{L;\tau}=\gamma_{\tau}\breve{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau},\quad\text{ with }\gamma_{\tau}:=\frac{\epsilon}{D_{\tau}\sqrt{2\mathfrak{c}\log(2/\delta)}},

(S.61)

\mathfrak{c}>0
,
W^{(j)}_{\tau}\sim N(0,1)
independent for
j=1,\dots,m
and
\tau>0
. These transcripts are
(\epsilon/{\mathfrak{c}},\delta)
-differentially private for any
\epsilon>0
(see e.g.
[
36
]
). Define the test

\varphi_{\tau}:=\text{1}\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{L;\tau}\geq\left(\gamma_{\tau}\vee 1\right)\kappa\right\}.

(S.62)

We will prove the following more general version of Lemma
1
.

Lemma 21
.

Consider the test
\varphi_{\tau}
as defined by (
17
). Whenever
\tau/4\leq\frac{n\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{d_{L}}}\leq\tau/2
and

\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\geq C_{\alpha}\kappa\sigma^{2}\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}\log(1/\delta)}\log(N)\left(\frac{\sqrt{d_{L}}}{\sqrt{N}\sqrt{n}(\sqrt{n}\epsilon\wedge 1)}\right)\bigvee\left(\frac{1}{Nn\epsilon^{2}}\right),

(S.63)

for
C_{\alpha}>0
large enough, it holds that
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-\varphi_{\tau})\leq\alpha
.

On the event that
X^{(j)}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{\tau}
for all
j\in[m]
, we have that

\sum_{j=1}^{m}Y^{(j)}_{\tau}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\gamma_{\tau}\breve{S}_{\tau}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\gamma_{\tau}S_{\tau}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau}\right).

(S.64)

Consequently,
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-\varphi_{\tau})
is bounded above by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\gamma_{\tau}S_{\tau}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau}\right)\geq\left(\gamma_{\tau}\vee 1\right)\kappa\right)+{\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(\exists j:X^{(j)}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{\tau}\right).

(S.65)

By Lemma
18
and a union bound, the second term is bounded above by
\alpha/2
.

Under
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}
, it holds that

\frac{n}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\left(\left\|\sigma^{-1}\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}\right\|^{2}_{2}-\frac{V^{(j)}}{n}\right)\overset{d}{=}\frac{n\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}+2\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\langle Z,\sigma^{-1}f\rangle+\frac{\|Z\|_{2}^{2}-V^{(j)}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}},

(S.66)

where
Z\sim N(0,I_{d_{L}})
. By assumption,
\frac{n\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}\sigma^{2}}\leq\tau/2
,
\text{Var}(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\langle Z,\sigma^{-1}f_{L}\rangle)=n\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}/d_{L}\leq\tau/2
and
({\|Z\|_{2}^{2}-V^{(j)}})/{\sqrt{d_{L}}}
tends to a Gaussian with variance
4
for large
d_{L}
. The second and third term in (
S.66
) are symmetric in distribution about
0
, have uniformly bounded densities (since the Chi-square and normal densities are bounded, and the third term tends weakly to a Gaussian in
d_{L}
) and
\sigma^{-2}d^{-1/2}_{L}n\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\tau/2
, which means that the conditions of Lemma
28
are satisfied. Applying said lemma (with
\mu=\sigma^{-2}d^{-1/2}_{L}n\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}
), we get that there exists a uniform constant
c>0
such that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}_{f}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\gamma_{\tau}S_{\tau}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau}\right)\geq c\frac{\sqrt{m}n\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\gamma_{\tau}}{\sigma^{2}\sqrt{d_{L}}}.

Under
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}
, by independence of the data and the Gaussian noise,

\displaystyle\text{Var}_{f}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\gamma_{\tau}S_{\tau}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau}\right)

\displaystyle=1+\text{Var}_{f}\left(\gamma_{\tau}S_{\tau}(X^{(1)})\right).

Since

{\mathbb{E}}_{f}\frac{n}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\left(\left\|\sigma^{-1}\overline{X^{(j)}}_{L}\right\|^{2}_{2}-\frac{V^{(j)}}{n}\right)=\frac{n\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\leq\tau/2,

Lemma
29
yields

\displaystyle\text{Var}_{f}\left(\gamma_{\tau}S_{\tau}(X^{(1)})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{\tau}^{2}\text{Var}_{f}\left(\frac{n}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\left(\left\|\sigma^{-1}\overline{X^{(j)}}\right\|^{2}_{2}-\frac{V^{(j)}}{n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\gamma_{\tau}^{2}\left(\frac{4n\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}d_{L}}+4\right).

Assume now that for all
C_{\alpha}>0
large enough,

\left(\gamma_{\tau}\vee 1\right)\kappa\leq c\frac{1}{2}{\sqrt{m}n\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\gamma_{\tau}}/\sqrt{d_{L}},

(S.67)

which is a claim we shall prove later on. Then, the first term in (
S.65
) is bounded above by

{\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\left(\gamma_{\tau}S_{\tau}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau}-{\mathbb{E}}_{f}(S_{\tau}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau})\right)<-c\frac{\sqrt{m}n\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\gamma_{\tau}}{2\sqrt{d_{L}}}\right),

(S.68)

which, by Chebyshev’s inequality is bounded by

\displaystyle\left(c\frac{\sqrt{m}n\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\gamma_{\tau}}{2\sqrt{d_{L}}}\right)^{-2}\left({1+\gamma_{\tau}^{2}\left(\frac{4\sigma^{-2}n\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}{d_{L}}+4\right)}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\sqrt{m}n\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\gamma_{\tau}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\right)^{-2}+\left({{m}n\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}\right)^{-1}+\left(\frac{\sqrt{m}n\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\right)^{-2}.\;\;

For
f
satisfying (
19
), the last two terms are easily seen to be smaller than
\alpha/6
for a large enough to choice for
C_{\alpha}
. To see that this is also true for the first term, recall that
\gamma_{\tau}:=\frac{\epsilon}{D_{\tau}\sqrt{2\mathfrak{c}\log(2/\delta)}}
, with

D_{\tau}=\frac{\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\log(N)(\sqrt{n\sqrt{d_{L}}\tau}\vee\sqrt{nd_{L}})}{n\sqrt{d_{L}}},

which yields that the square root of the first term equals

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{m}n\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\epsilon}{\sqrt{d_{L}}D_{\tau}\sqrt{2\mathfrak{c}\log(2/\delta)}}=\frac{\sqrt{m}n^{2}\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\epsilon}{\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\log(N)(\sqrt{n\sqrt{d_{L}}\tau}\vee\sqrt{nd_{L}})\sqrt{2\mathfrak{c}\log(2/\delta)}}.

When the maximum is taken in
\sqrt{nd_{L}}
, (
S.63
) leads to the latter being larger than
C_{\alpha}
. When the maximum is taken in
\sqrt{n\sqrt{d_{L}}\tau}
, using that
4\sigma^{-2}n\|f\|_{2}^{2}/\sqrt{d_{L}}\geq\tau
yields that the above display is bounded from below by

\frac{\sqrt{m}\sigma^{-1}n\|f_{L}\|_{2}\epsilon}{\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\sqrt{\log(N)\sqrt{2\mathfrak{c}\log(2/\delta)}}}\geq C_{\alpha}.

In either case, it follows that the Type II error (i.e. (
S.68
)) can be made arbitrarily small per large enough choice of
C_{\alpha}>0
.

We return to the claim of (
S.67
). When
\gamma_{\tau}\geq 1
, the claim is satisfied whenever
\kappa\lesssim\log(N)\sqrt{\mathfrak{c}\log(1/\delta)}
for
C_{\alpha}>0
large enough. When
\gamma_{\tau}<1
, it is required that

\frac{\sqrt{m}\sigma^{-2}n\gamma_{\tau}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\epsilon}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}=\frac{\sqrt{m}\sigma^{-2}n^{2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\epsilon}{\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\log(N)\sqrt{2\mathfrak{c}\log(2/\delta)}(\sqrt{n\sqrt{d_{L}}\tau}\vee\sqrt{nd_{L}})}\gtrsim\kappa,

which is satisfied whenever (
S.63
) holds.
∎

B.1.2
Proof of Lemma
2

We start by recalling the notation, before proving a slightly more general result. Consider for a set
{\mathcal{S}}\subset{\mathbb{N}}
the test

T_{\text{I}}:=\underset{L\in{\mathcal{S}},\,\tau\in\mathrm{T}_{L}}{\max}\text{1}\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{L;\tau}\geq\kappa_{\alpha}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{D_{\tau}\sqrt{2|\mathrm{T}_{L}||{\mathcal{S}}|\log(2/\delta)}}\vee 1\right)\sqrt{\log|\mathrm{T}_{L}||{\mathcal{S}}|}\right\},

(S.69)

where

\mathrm{T}_{L}:=\left\{2^{-k+2}\frac{nR^{2}}{\sqrt{2^{L+1}}}:k=1,\dots,\lceil 1+2\log_{2}(NR/\sigma)\rceil\right\},

(S.70)

and
Y^{(j)}_{L}=\{Y^{(j)}_{L;\tau}:\tau\in\mathrm{T}_{L}\}
is generated according to (
S.61
) with

\gamma_{\tau}=\frac{\epsilon}{D_{\tau}\sqrt{2|\mathrm{T}_{L}||{\mathcal{S}}|\log(2/\delta)}}.

By the same reasoning as in the proof of Lemma
21
, the test
T_{\text{I}}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-differentially private. We will prove the following more general version of Lemma
2
.

Lemma 22
.

For all
M>0
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
there exists
\kappa_{\alpha}>0
and
C_{\alpha}>0
such that the test defined by (
S.69
) satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T_{\text{I}}\leq\alpha
.

Furthermore, for any
f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{{s},R}_{p,q}
such that for some
L^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}
it holds that

\|f_{L^{*}}\|_{2}^{2}\geq C_{\alpha}M_{N}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2^{L^{*}}}}{\sqrt{m}n(\sqrt{n}\epsilon\wedge 1)}\right)\bigvee\left(\frac{1}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right),

(S.71)

where

M_{N}\gtrsim\log(N)\sqrt{2\log\log(NR/\sigma)\log(NR/\sigma)|{\mathcal{S}}|\log(2/\delta)},

it holds that
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T_{\text{I}})\leq\alpha
for
C_{\alpha}>0
large enough depending only on
\alpha
.

We start by proving that the level of the test is controlled. Using a union bound, it holds that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T_{\text{I}}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{P}}_{0}\left(\underset{L\in{\mathcal{S}},\;\tau\in\mathrm{T}_{L}}{\max}\,\frac{1}{\left(\gamma_{\tau}\vee 1\right)\sqrt{\log|\mathrm{T}||{\mathcal{S}}|}\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}[\gamma_{L}\breve{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau}]\geq\kappa_{\alpha}\left(\gamma_{\tau}\vee 1\right)\sqrt{\log|\mathrm{T}_{L}||{\mathcal{S}}|}\right)

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\left(\underset{L\in{\mathcal{S}},\,\tau\in\mathrm{T}_{L}}{\max}\;\frac{1}{\left(\gamma_{\tau}\vee 1\right)\sqrt{\log|\mathrm{T}||{\mathcal{S}}|}\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}[\gamma_{L}\tilde{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau}]\geq\kappa_{\alpha}\right)

(S.72)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\left(\exists j\in[m],\,L\in{\mathcal{S}},\,\tau\in\mathrm{T}_{L}\,:\,X^{(j)}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}\right)

where it is used that the Lipschitz extension
\breve{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}
of
\tilde{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}
equals the latter function on
{\mathcal{C}}_{L;\tau}
. By Lemma
18
and a union bound, the second probability on the right-hand side is bounded above by
\alpha m|{\mathcal{S}}||\mathrm{T}_{L}|/(2N)
, for a large enough choice of
\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}>0
.

Considering the first probability on the right-hand side of the inequality displayed above, we first note that by Lemma
27
,

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}(X^{(j)})

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\left(\left\|\sqrt{n}\overline{X^{(j)}_{L}}\right\|^{2}_{2}-{V^{(j)}_{L;\tau}}\right)\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}

is
1/(4\sqrt{d})
-sub-exponential, where the sub-exponentiality parameter follows from a straightforward calculation (see e.g. Lemma
[
76
]
). Since
W^{(j)}_{L;\tau}
is independent
N(0,1)
, it follows by Bernstein’s inequality (see e.g. Theorem 2.8.1 in
[
83
]
) and a union bound (e.g. Lemma
31
) that the first probability in (
S.72
) is bounded as follows

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\left(\underset{L\in{\mathcal{S}},\,\tau\in\mathrm{T}_{L}}{\max}\;\frac{1}{\left(\gamma_{\tau}\vee 1\right)\sqrt{\log|\mathrm{T}||{\mathcal{S}}|}\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}[\gamma_{L}\tilde{S}_{L;\tau}^{(j)}(X^{(j)})+W^{(j)}_{\tau}]\geq\kappa_{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{L\in{\mathcal{S}},\,\tau\in\mathrm{T}_{L}}\frac{1}{(|\mathrm{T}_{L}||{\mathcal{S}}|)^{\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}/2}},

which is less than
\alpha/2
for a large enough choice of
\kappa_{\alpha}>0
. For
f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{{s},R}_{p,q}
, Lemma
41
yields that

\displaystyle\|f_{L}\|_{2}\leq\|f\|_{2}\leq\frac{1}{(1-2^{-s})^{1-1/q}}\|f\|_{s,p,q}\leq\frac{R}{(1-2^{-s})^{1-1/q}}.

(S.73)

Consequently, when
f
satisfies (
19
), it holds in particular that
\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\geq N^{-1}
. Thus, there exists
\tau^{*}\in\mathrm{T}_{L^{*}}
such that the condition of Lemma
21
is satisfied, which now yields that

{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T_{\text{I}})\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-\varphi_{\tau^{*}})\leq\alpha/2.

∎

B.2
Procedure II

Let
\epsilon,\delta>0
and
{\mathcal{S}}\subset{\mathbb{N}}
be given. Consider for
L\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
K_{L}=\lceil n\epsilon^{2}\wedge d_{L}\rceil
and take sets
{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}\subset[m]
such that
|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|=\lceil\frac{mK_{L}}{d_{L}}\rceil
and each
j\in\{1,\dots,m\}
is in
{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}
for
K_{L}
different indexes
k\in\{1,\dots,d_{L}\}
. For
(l,k)\in\{l=1,\dots,L,k=1,\dots,2^{l}\}=:I_{L}
,
j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}
, generate the transcripts according to

Y^{(j)}_{L;lk}|X^{(j)}=\gamma_{L}\,\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\sigma^{-1}\left[(X_{L;i}^{(j)})_{lk}\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}+W^{(j)}_{L;lk}

(S.74)

with
\gamma_{L}=\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{|{\mathcal{S}}|K_{L}\log(2/\delta)\tau}}
,
\tau=\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\sqrt{\log(N)}
and
(W^{(j)}_{L;lk})_{j,k}
i.i.d. standard Gaussian noise.

Define
Y^{(j)}_{L}:=(Y^{(j)}_{L;lk})_{(l,k)\in I_{L}:j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}
and consider the transcripts
Y^{(j)}:=(Y^{(j)}_{L})_{L\in{\mathcal{S}}}
, for
j=1,\ldots,m
. The lemma below shows that
Y^{(j)}
satisfies DP.

Lemma 23
.

The transcript defined
Y^{(j)}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-differentially private.

The rescaled and clipped sums have at most
L_{2}
-sensitivity less than or equal to one:

\displaystyle\underset{\breve{x}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{N}})^{n}:\mathrm{d}_{H}(x,\breve{x})\leq 1}{\sup}\,\gamma_{L}\left\|\left(\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\sigma^{-1}[(x_{i})_{lk}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}-\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\sigma^{-1}[(\breve{x}_{i})_{lk}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\right)_{(l,k)\in I_{L},L\in{\mathcal{S}}}\right\|_{2}\leq

(S.75)

\displaystyle\gamma_{L}\sqrt{\sum_{L\in{\mathcal{S}}}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left(\underset{\breve{x}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{N}})^{n}:\mathrm{d}_{H}(x,\breve{x})\leq 1}{\sup}\sigma^{-1}[(x_{i})_{lk}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}-\sigma^{-1}[(\breve{x}_{i})_{lk}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\right)^{2}}\leq 1.

Consequently, the addition of the Gaussian noise assures that the transcript
Y^{(j)}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-differentially private (see e.g. Appendix A in
[
36
]
).
∎

Consider the test given by

T_{\text{II}}:=\text{1}\left\{\underset{L\in{\mathcal{S}}}{\max}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}\left(n\gamma_{L}^{2}\vee 1\right)}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|}}\underset{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{L;lk}\right)^{2}-\nu_{\epsilon,L}\right]\geq\kappa_{\alpha}\log\log(e\vee|{\mathcal{S}}|)\right\},

(S.76)

with
\nu_{\epsilon,L}:={\mathbb{E}}_{0}(|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|^{-1/2}\sum_{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}Y^{(j)}_{L;lk})^{2}
.

Lemma 24
.

For all
M>0
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
there exists
\kappa_{\alpha}>0
and
C_{\alpha}>0
such that the test defined by (
S.76
) satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T_{\text{II}}\leq\alpha
. Furthermore, for any
f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{{s},R}_{p,q}
such that

\|f_{L^{*}}\|_{2}^{2}\geq C_{\alpha}\log\log(e\vee|{\mathcal{S}}|)\log(N)|{\mathcal{S}}|\log(1/\delta)\left(\frac{\sqrt{2^{(3/2)L^{*}}}}{\sqrt{m}n({n}\epsilon^{2}\wedge d_{L^{*}})}\right)\bigvee\left(\frac{1}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right),

(S.77)

for some
L^{*}\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
C_{\alpha}>0
large enough depending only on
\alpha
, it holds that
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T_{\text{II}})\leq\alpha
.

Under the null hypothesis,
(X_{L;i}^{(j)})_{lk}
are independent standard Gaussian for
(l,k)\in I_{L}
,
j\in[m]
. Hence, by Lemma
27
, the random variables
Y^{(j)}_{L;lk}
are sub-gaussian, mean zero and i.i.d. for
k\in[d_{L}]
,
j\in[m]
under the null hypothesis. More specifically, we have that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|}}\underset{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{L;lk}\quad\text{ is }\quad\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}\epsilon}{2\sqrt{|{\mathcal{S}}|K_{L}\log(2/\delta)\tau}}+1\right)\text{-sub-gaussian},

which in turn implies that the random variables

\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}\left(n\gamma_{L}^{2}\vee 1\right)}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|}}\underset{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{L;lk}\right)^{2}-\nu_{\epsilon,L}\right]

are
C
-sub-exponential for
L\in{\mathcal{S}}
, for some constant
C>0
(see e.g. Section 2.7 in
[
83
]
). It now follows from Lemma
31
that, for
\kappa_{\alpha}>0
large enough,
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T_{\text{II}}\leq\alpha/2
.

We now turn our attention to the Type II error. Assume now that, for
L\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
f
satisfies (
S.77
). We have that

1-T_{\text{II}}\leq\text{1}\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}\left(n\gamma_{L}^{2}\vee 1\right)}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|}}\underset{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{L;lk}\right)^{2}-\nu_{\epsilon,L}\right]<\kappa_{\alpha}\log\log(e\vee|{\mathcal{S}}|\right\}),

so it suffices to bound the
{\mathbb{E}}_{f}
-expectation of the right-hand side.

Under
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}
,
(X_{i}^{(j)})_{lk}\overset{d}{=}f_{lk}+\sigma^{-1}Z_{lk;i}^{(j)}
with i.i.d.
Z_{lk;i}^{(j)}\sim N(0,1)
and is independent of the centered i.i.d.
W^{(j)}_{lk}
. Hence, the quantity

V_{lk}:=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|}}\underset{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\gamma_{L}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\sigma^{-1}(X_{i}^{(j)})_{lk}+W^{(j)}_{lk}\right]\right)^{2}

is in distribution equal to

\left(\gamma_{L}\sqrt{|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|}n\sigma^{-1}f_{lk}+\gamma_{L}\sqrt{n}\eta+\frac{1}{\sqrt{|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|}}\underset{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}W^{(j)}_{lk}\right)^{2}

under
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}
, with
\eta\sim N(0,1)
independent. Therefore, a straightforward calculation shows that
V_{lk}
has mean
\sigma^{-1}\gamma_{L}^{2}n^{2}|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|f_{lk}^{2}+n\gamma_{L}^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}(W^{(j)}_{lk})^{2}
under
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}
. Since
{\mathbb{E}}(Z_{lk;i}^{(j)})^{4}=3
and
{\mathbb{E}}(W^{(j)}_{lk})^{4}\asymp 1
, its variance equals

\displaystyle n^{2}\gamma_{L}^{4}\text{Var}\left(\eta^{2}\right)+\text{Var}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|}}\underset{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}W^{(j)}_{lk}\right)^{2}\right)+\gamma_{L}^{4}|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|n^{3}\sigma^{-2}f_{lk}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\eta^{2}

(S.78)

\displaystyle+\gamma_{L}^{2}|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|n^{2}\sigma^{-2}f_{lk}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}(W^{(j)}_{l})^{2}+n\gamma_{L}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}(W^{(j)}_{lk})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\eta^{2},

which is of the order

(\gamma_{L}^{4}|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|n^{3}\sigma^{-2}f_{lk}^{2})\vee(\gamma_{L}^{2}|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|n^{2}\sigma^{-2}f_{lk}^{2})\vee\gamma_{L}^{4}n^{2}\vee 1.

(S.79)

Since (
S.73
) holds, we have that for
\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}
large enough,

\underset{((l,k)\in I_{L})}{\max}|f_{lk}|\leq\tau/2.

(S.80)

Consequently, an application of the triangle inequality and Lemma
34
yield that, for
\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}>0
large enough, we have with probability at least
1-2Nd_{l}e^{-\tau^{2}/4}\geq 1-(1+N)^{2-\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}^{2}/4}\geq 1-\alpha/4
that

\underset{i\in[n],j\in[m],(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\max}|(X_{i}^{(j)})_{lk}|\leq\tau.

Consequently, under the null hypothesis (
f=0
), using that
|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|=|{\mathcal{J}}_{1}|
,
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\varphi
is bounded above by

{\mathbb{P}}_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}|{\mathcal{J}}_{1d_{L};L}|}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\left(\underset{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left(\gamma_{L}\sigma^{-1}(n\bar{X^{(j)}})_{lk}+W^{(j)}_{lk}\right)\right)^{2}-{\mathbb{E}}_{0}V_{l}\right]\geq\kappa(\gamma_{L}^{2}n\vee 1)\right)+\frac{\alpha}{4},

where we write
\kappa=\kappa_{\alpha}\log\log(e\vee|{\mathcal{S}}|)
. Chebyshev’s inequality yields that the first term on the left-hand side is bounded
\alpha/4
for
\kappa_{\alpha}>0
large enough. In the case that (
S.86
) holds, we also have that
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T_{\text{II}})
is bounded above by

\text{Pr}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}|{\mathcal{J}}_{1d_{L};L}|}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\left(\underset{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left(\gamma_{L}(nf_{lk}+\sqrt{n}Z)+W^{(j)}_{lk}\right)\right)^{2}-{\mathbb{E}}_{0}V_{l}\right]<\kappa(\gamma_{L}^{2}n\vee 1)\right)+\frac{\alpha}{4}.

Subtracting
d^{-1/2}_{L}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\gamma_{L}^{2}n^{2}|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|\sigma^{-1}f_{lk}^{2}
on both sides, the first term is bounded above by

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\bigg(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{L}}|{\mathcal{J}}_{1d_{L};L}|}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\left(\underset{j\in{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left(\gamma_{L}(n\sigma^{-1}f_{lk}+\sqrt{n}Z)+W^{(j)}_{l}\right)\right)^{2}-{\mathbb{E}}_{f}V_{l}\right]

\displaystyle<

\displaystyle-\frac{\gamma_{L}^{2}n^{2}|{\mathcal{J}}_{1d_{L};L}|\sigma^{-2}\|f\|_{2}^{2}}{2\sqrt{d_{L}}}\bigg)

whenever

\sigma^{-2}\frac{\gamma_{L}^{2}n^{2}|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\geq 2\kappa(\gamma_{L}^{2}n\vee 1)\iff\frac{2\sigma^{2}\kappa_{\alpha}\log\log(e\vee|{\mathcal{S}}|(\gamma_{L}^{2}n\vee 1)d_{L}\sqrt{d_{L}}}{\gamma_{L}^{2})n^{2}mK_{L}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}\leq 1.

This follows from the assumed inequality (
S.77
) in the lemma’s statement.

An application of Chebyshev’s inequality and the variance bound computed in (
S.79
), now yields that the probability in the second last display is of the order

\frac{(d^{-1}_{L}\gamma_{L}^{4}|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|n^{3}\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2})\vee(d^{-1}_{L}\gamma_{L}^{2}|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|n^{2}\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2})\vee\gamma_{L}^{4}n^{2}\vee 1}{\left(\frac{\gamma_{L}^{2}n^{2}|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|}{\sqrt{d_{L}}}\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}}.

Plugging in
|{\mathcal{J}}_{lk;L}|\asymp mK_{L}/d_{L}
,
\gamma_{L}=\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{|{\mathcal{S}}|K_{L}\log(2/\delta)\tau}}
and
\tau=\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\sqrt{\log(N)}
, we obtain that the latter display equals

\frac{(\frac{m\epsilon^{4}}{16K_{L}d_{L}\Lambda_{N,\delta,|{\mathcal{S}}|}^{2}}n^{3}\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2})\vee(\frac{m\epsilon^{2}}{4K_{L}d_{L}\Lambda_{N,\delta,|{\mathcal{S}}|}}n^{2}\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2})\vee(\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{16K_{L}\Lambda_{N,\delta,|{\mathcal{S}}|}^{2}})n^{2}\vee 1}{\left(\frac{m\epsilon^{2}n^{2}}{4K_{L}d_{L}\Lambda_{N,\delta,|{\mathcal{S}}|}\sqrt{d_{L}}}\sigma^{-2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\right)^{2}}

(S.81)

with
\Lambda_{N,\delta,|{\mathcal{S}}|}=|{\mathcal{S}}|\log(2/\delta)\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}^{2}{\log(N)}
. For
1/\sqrt{n}\lesssim\epsilon
and
n\epsilon^{2}\lesssim d_{L}
, this expression is of the order

\frac{\Lambda_{N,\delta,|{\mathcal{S}}|}^{2}d^{3}_{L}}{m^{2}n^{4}\epsilon^{4}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{4}},

which is bounded by
{1}/{C_{\alpha}^{2}}
when
f
satisfies (
S.77
). This can be seen to be arbitrarily small whenever
f
is such that (
S.77
) by taking
C_{\alpha}>0
large enough. Lastly, when
n\epsilon^{2}\gtrsim d_{L}
, (
S.89
) is of the order

\frac{\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}\Lambda_{N,\delta,|{\mathcal{S}}|}d_{L}}{m^{2}n^{2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{4}},

(S.82)

which also holds for
C_{\alpha}>0
large enough for
f
satisfying (
S.77
). Consequently, we obtain that
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T_{\text{II}}^{\epsilon,\delta})\leq\alpha/2
for
C_{\alpha}
large enough, as desired.

∎

B.3
Procedure III

Let
{\mathcal{S}}\subset{\mathbb{N}}
and consider for
L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
d_{L}:=\sum_{l=1}^{L}2^{l}
,
K_{L}:=\lceil n\epsilon^{2}\wedge 2^{L}\rceil
,
I_{L}:=\{(l,k):l=1,\dots,\lceil\log_{2}(K_{L})\rceil,\,k=1,\dots,2^{l}\}
and
j=1,\dots,m
the transcripts

Y^{(j)}_{lk}|(X^{(j)},U_{L})=\gamma_{L}\,\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}[(U_{L}X_{L;i}^{(j)})_{lk}]^{\tau}_{-\tau}+W^{(j)}_{lk},

(S.83)

with
\gamma_{L}=\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{K_{L}|{\mathcal{S}}|\log(2/\delta)\tau}}
,
\tau=\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\sqrt{\log(N)}
,
U_{L}
a
d_{L}
-by-
d_{L}
random rotation drawn uniformly (i.e. from the Haar measure on the special orthogonal group in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}\times d_{L}}
) and
(W^{(j)}_{lk})_{j,l,k}
i.i.d. centered standard Gaussian noise. The following lemma shows that the transcript is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-differentially private.

Lemma 25
.

The transcript defined in (
S.83
) is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-differentially private.

The argument is similar to that of Lemma
23
, noting that rescaled and clipped sums have at most
L_{2}
-sensitivity less than or equal to one for any rotation
U_{L}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d_{L}\times d_{L}}
;

\displaystyle\underset{\breve{x}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{N}})^{n}:\mathrm{d}_{H}(x,\breve{x})\leq 1}{\sup}\,\gamma_{L}\left\|\left(\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\sigma^{-1}[U_{L}(x_{i})_{lk}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}-\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}\sigma^{-1}[U_{L}(\breve{x}_{i})_{lk}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\right)_{(l,k)\in I_{L},L\in{\mathcal{S}}}\right\|_{2}\leq

\displaystyle\gamma_{L}\sqrt{\sum_{L\in{\mathcal{S}}}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left(\underset{\breve{x}\in({\mathbb{R}}^{\mathbb{N}})^{n}:\mathrm{d}_{H}(x,\breve{x})\leq 1}{\sup}\sigma^{-1}[U_{L}(x_{i})_{lk}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}-\sigma^{-1}[U_{L}(\breve{x}_{i})_{lk}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\right)^{2}}\leq 1.

so by the same reasoning as in Lemma
23
, the transcript
Y^{(j)}
is
(\epsilon,\delta)
-differentially private.
∎

Based on these transcripts, one can compute

T_{\text{III}}=\text{1}\left\{\underset{L\in{\mathcal{L}}}{\max}\;\frac{1}{\sqrt{K_{L}}\left(n\gamma_{L}^{2}\vee 1\right)}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{lk}\right)^{2}-\nu_{L}\right]\geq\kappa_{\alpha}\log\log(e\vee|{\mathcal{S}}|)\right\},

(S.84)

where
\nu_{L}=n\gamma_{L}^{2}+1
. The following lemma establishes the Type I and Type II error probability guarantees for the above test.

Lemma 26
.

For all
s,R>0
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
there exists
\kappa_{\alpha},\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}>0
and
C_{\alpha}>0
such that the test
T_{\text{III}}
defined by (
S.84
) satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T_{\text{III}}\leq\alpha
. Furthermore, for any
f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{{s},R}_{p,q}
such that

\|f_{L^{*}}\|_{2}^{2}\geq C_{\alpha}\log\log(e\vee|{\mathcal{S}}|)\log(N)|{\mathcal{S}}|\log(1/\delta)\left(\frac{2^{L^{*}}}{\sqrt{m}n(\sqrt{n}\epsilon\wedge 1)}\right)\bigvee\left(\frac{1}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right),

(S.85)

for some
L^{*}\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
C_{\alpha}>0
large enough depending only on
\alpha
, it holds that
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T_{\text{II}}^{\epsilon,\delta})\leq\alpha
.

Under
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}
,
(U_{L}X_{L;i}^{(j)})_{lk}\overset{d}{=}(U_{L}f)_{lk}+(U_{L}Z_{L;i}^{(j)})_{lk}
, where
Z_{L;i}^{(j)}\sim N(0,I_{d_{L}})
independent of
U_{L}
and the centered i.i.d.
W^{(j)}_{lk}
. Furthermore,
U_{L}Z_{L;i}^{(j)}\overset{d}{=}Z_{L;i}^{(j)}
,
(U_{L}Z_{L;i}^{(j)})_{lk}\sim N(0,1)
, still independent of
(W^{(j)}_{lk})_{l,k,j}
. We obtain that

\gamma_{L}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}(U_{L}X_{L;i}^{(j)})_{lk}+W^{(j)}_{lk}\overset{d}{=}\gamma_{L}n(U_{L}f_{L})_{lk}+\gamma_{L}\sqrt{n}\eta+W^{(j)}_{lk},

with
\eta\sim N(0,1)
and all three terms independent.

A first consequence of the relationship above is that under the null hypothesis, the random variables
Y^{(j)}_{L;k}
are sub-gaussian, mean zero and i.i.d. for
(l,k)\in I_{L}
,
j\in[m]
under the null hypothesis (see Lemma
27
). More specifically, we have that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{lk}\quad\text{ is }\quad\left(\frac{\sqrt{n}\epsilon}{2\sqrt{|{\mathcal{S}}|{K_{L}}\log(2/\delta)\tau}}+1\right)\text{-sub-gaussian},

which in turn implies that the random variables

\frac{1}{\sqrt{K_{L}}}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{L;k}\right)^{2}-\nu_{L}\right]

are
C
-sub-exponential for
L\in{\mathcal{S}}
, for some constant
C>0
(see e.g. Section 2.7 in
[
83
]
). It now follows from Lemma
31
that, for
\kappa_{\alpha}>0
large enough,
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T_{\text{III}}\leq\alpha/2
.

Next, we turn to the Type II error probability. Assume now that, for
L\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
f
satisfies (
S.77
). We have that

1-T_{\text{III}}\leq\text{1}\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{K_{L}}}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{lk}\right)^{2}-\nu_{L}\right]<\kappa_{\alpha}\left(n\gamma_{L}^{2}\vee 1\right)\log\log(e\vee|{\mathcal{S}}|)\right\},

so it suffices to bound the
{\mathbb{E}}_{f}
-expectation of the right-hand side. Since (
S.73
) holds, we have that for
\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}
large enough,

\underset{((l,k)\in I_{L})}{\max}|f_{lk}|\leq\tau/2.

(S.86)

Consequently, an application of the triangle inequality and Lemma
34
yield that, for
\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}>0
large enough, we have with probability at least
1-2Nd_{l}e^{-\tau^{2}/4}\geq 1-(1+N)^{2-\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}^{2}/4}\geq 1-\alpha/4
that

\underset{i\in[n],j\in[m],(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\max}|(X_{i}^{(j)})_{lk}|\leq\tau.

Thus, it suffices to show that

{\mathbb{P}}_{f}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{K_{L}}}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}Y^{(j)}_{lk}\right)^{2}-\nu_{L}\right]<\kappa_{\alpha}\left(n\gamma_{L}^{2}\vee 1\right)\log\log(e\vee|{\mathcal{S}}|)\right)\leq\alpha/4,

(S.87)

for
C_{\alpha}>0
large enough. Define

V_{lk}:=\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}\left[\gamma_{L}\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}(U_{L}X_{i}^{(j)})_{lk}+W^{(j)}_{lk}\right]\right)^{2}.

Under
{\mathbb{P}}^{U}
, we have that

(U_{L}f_{L})_{lk}\overset{d}{=}\|f_{L}\|_{2}\frac{Z_{lk}}{\|Z\|_{2}},

for
Z=(Z_{11},\dots,Z_{1d_{L}})\sim N(0,I_{d_{L}})
. As
\sum_{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\mathbb{E}}{Z_{lk}^{2}}/{\|Z\|_{2}^{2}}=1
,
{\mathbb{E}}{Z_{lk}^{2}}/{\|Z\|_{2}^{2}}=1/d_{L}
by symmetry. Hence,

{\mathbb{E}}_{f}V_{lk}=\gamma_{L}^{2}mn^{2}{\mathbb{E}}^{U}(U_{L}f_{L})_{lk}^{2}+n\gamma_{L}^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}(W^{(j)}_{lk})^{2}=\frac{\gamma_{L}^{2}mn^{2}\sqrt{K_{L}}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}{d_{L}}+n\gamma_{L}^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}(W^{(j)}_{lk})^{2}.

Subtracting
d^{-1}\gamma_{L}^{2}mn^{2}\sqrt{K_{L}}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}
on both sides, the first term in (
S.87
) is bounded above by

\text{Pr}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{K_{L}}}\underset{(l,k)\in I_{L}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\left[V_{lk}-{\mathbb{E}}_{f}V_{lk}\right]<-\frac{\gamma_{L}^{2}n^{2}m\sqrt{K_{L}}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}{2{d_{L}}}\right)

whenever

\frac{\gamma_{L}^{2}mn^{2}\sqrt{K_{L}}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}{{d_{L}}}\geq 2\kappa_{\alpha}(\gamma_{L}^{2}n\vee 1).

(S.88)

The latter is indeed satisfied whenever (
S.85
). Under the alternative hypothesis, a straightforward calculation shows that
V_{l}
has expectation conditionally on
U_{L}
equal to
\gamma_{L}^{2}mn^{2}(U_{L}f)_{lk}^{2}+n\gamma_{L}^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}(W^{(j)}_{lk})^{2}
. Since
{\mathbb{E}}(Z_{L;i})_{lk}^{4}=3
and
{\mathbb{E}}(W^{(j)}_{lk})^{4}\asymp 1
, its variance conditionally on
U_{L}
equals

\displaystyle n^{2}\gamma_{L}^{4}

\displaystyle\text{Var}\left(\eta^{2}\big|U_{L}\right)+\text{Var}\left(\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}W^{(j)}_{lk}\right)^{2}\bigg|U_{L}\right)+2\gamma_{L}^{4}n^{3}m(U_{L}f)_{lk}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\eta^{2}

\displaystyle+2\gamma_{L}^{2}mn^{2}(U_{L}f)_{lk}^{2}{\mathbb{E}}(W^{(j)}_{lk})^{2}+2\gamma_{L}^{2}n{\mathbb{E}}(W^{(j)}_{lk})^{2}{\mathbb{E}}\eta^{2},

which is of the order

(\gamma_{L}^{4}mn^{3}(U_{L}f)_{lk}^{2})\vee(\gamma_{L}^{2}mn^{2}(U_{L}f)_{lk}^{2})\vee\gamma_{L}^{4}n^{2}\vee 1.

Consequently, by applying Chebyshev’s inequality, the probability on the left-hand side of (
S.87
) is of the order

{\left(\frac{d_{L}}{mn\sqrt{K_{L}}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\vee\left(\frac{d_{L}\Lambda_{N,{\mathcal{S}},\delta}\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}^{2}}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\vee\left(\frac{\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}d^{2}_{L}}{m^{2}n^{2}K_{L}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{4}}\right)\vee\left(\frac{\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}^{4}\Lambda_{N,{\mathcal{S}},\delta}^{2}d^{2}_{L}}{m^{2}n^{4}\epsilon^{4}K_{L}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{4}}\right)},

(S.89)

where
\Lambda_{N,{\mathcal{S}},\delta}:=\log(N)|{\mathcal{S}}|\log(1/\delta)\log
, and we have plugged in
\gamma_{L}=\frac{\epsilon}{2\sqrt{K_{L}|{\mathcal{S}}|\log(2/\delta)\tau}}
,
\tau=\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}\sqrt{\log(N)}
.
The latter can be made arbitrarily small per choice of
C_{\alpha}>0
in (
S.85
).

To see this, first consider the case where
\epsilon\leq n^{-1/2}
, such that
K_{L}\asymp 1
and the above display is of the order

\left(\frac{d_{L}\Lambda_{N,{\mathcal{S}},\delta}\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}^{2}}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\vee\left(\frac{\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}^{4}\Lambda_{N,{\mathcal{S}},\delta}^{2}d^{2}_{L}}{K_{L}m^{2}n^{4}\epsilon^{4}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{4}}\right)

Whenever
f
satisfies (
S.85
), the first term is of the order
1/C_{\alpha}
.

For
1/\sqrt{n}\leq\epsilon\leq d_{L}
,
K_{L}\asymp n\epsilon^{2}\wedge d_{L}\gtrsim 1
and (
S.89
) reduces to

\frac{\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}\tilde{\kappa}_{\alpha}^{4}\Lambda_{N,{\mathcal{S}},\delta}^{2}d^{2}_{L}}{m^{2}n^{3}\epsilon^{2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{4}},

When (
S.85
) holds, the above display is bounded by
1/C_{\alpha}^{2}
.

Lastly, when
n\epsilon^{2}\gtrsim d_{L}
, it holds that
K_{L}\asymp d_{L}
, so (
S.89
) is of the order
{\kappa_{\alpha}^{2}d_{L}}/({m^{2}n^{2}\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{4}})
. When (
S.85
) holds, the above display is bounded by
1/C_{\alpha}^{2}
. We conclude that
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T_{\text{III}})\leq\alpha/4
for
C_{\alpha}
large enough, as desired.
∎

B.4
Proofs of the theorems in Section
2
and Theorem
4

The proofs of Theorems
2
and
1
in Section
2
are direct consequences of Theorem
4
and
5
. To see this, note that for any sequences
m_{N}\equiv m
,
n_{N}\equiv N/m
,
\sigma_{N}\equiv\sigma
,
\epsilon_{N}\equiv\epsilon
and
\delta_{N}\equiv\delta
, Theorem
5
and Theorem
4
can be applied with an arbitrarily slow decreasing sequence
\alpha_{N}\to 0
or
\alpha_{N}\to 1
in order to obtain the desired convergence of the minimax risk.

Theorem
4
and Theorem
3
are consequences the lemmas proven earlier in the section. Below, we tie together the results of these lemmas to obtain both theorems.

Consider
{\mathcal{S}}=\{L\}
, for a given
L\in{\mathbb{N}}
which is to be determined. In the case of local randomness only protocols, let
T=T_{\text{I}}\vee T_{\text{II}}
, where
T_{\text{I}}
and
T_{\text{II}}
are the tests defined in (
S.69
) and (
S.76
), respectively, with their critical regions such that
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T_{\text{I}}\leq\alpha/4
and
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T_{\text{II}}\leq\alpha/4
(see the first statements of Lemma
22
and Lemma
24
). If, for some
L\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
f
satisfies

\left\|{f}_{L}\right\|_{2}^{2}\geq C_{\alpha}M_{N}\sigma^{2}\left(\frac{2^{(3/2)L}}{mn(n\epsilon^{2}\wedge 2^{L})}\bigwedge\left(\frac{\sqrt{2^{L}}}{\sqrt{m}n\sqrt{n\epsilon^{2}\wedge 1}}\bigvee\frac{1}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right)\right),

(S.90)

where

M_{N}\gtrsim\log(N)\sqrt{2\log\log(N)\log(N)|{\mathcal{S}}|\log(2/\delta)},

(S.91)

Lemma
22
and Lemma
24
yield that

{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T)\leq{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T_{\text{I}})+{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T_{\text{II}})\leq\alpha/2.

(S.92)

In view of
(a+b)^{2}/2-b^{2}\leq a^{2}
,

\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{\|f\|_{2}^{2}}{2}-\|f-f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}.

Since
f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{{s},R}_{p,q}
, we have that
\|f-f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\leq 2^{-2Ls}R^{2}
(see e.g. Lemma
44
). Consequently, taking
L=1\vee\lceil-\frac{1}{s}\log_{2}(\rho)\rceil
,

\|f_{L}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\rho^{2}M_{N}C_{\alpha}^{2}/2-R^{2}2^{-2Ls}\geq\rho M_{N}^{2}(C_{\alpha}^{2}/2-{R^{2}}).

We note here that the latter bound could be sharper by choosing
L=1\vee\lceil-\frac{1}{s}\log_{2}(\rho)\rceil
, but we choose the simpler bound for the sake of clarity. Given the above display, this choice of
L
means that (
S.90
) is satisfied for

\rho^{2}\gtrsim\sigma^{2}\left(\frac{(1\vee\rho^{-1/s})^{3/2}}{mn(n\epsilon^{2}\wedge(1\vee\rho^{-1/s}))}\bigwedge\left(\frac{\sqrt{1\vee\rho^{-1/s}}}{\sqrt{m}n\sqrt{n\epsilon^{2}\wedge 1}}\bigvee\frac{1}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right)\right).

(S.93)

Solving this for
\rho
, we obtain the rate in Theorem
4
, (
8
).

In case of shared randomness, the same argument applies, with the only difference that the test
T=T_{\text{I}}\vee T_{\text{III}}
is used. Lemmas
22
and
26
yield that this test satisfies
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T\leq\alpha/2
and also
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T)\leq\alpha/2
whenever

\rho^{2}\gtrsim\sigma^{2}\left(\frac{1\vee\rho^{-1/s}}{mn\sqrt{n\epsilon^{2}\wedge 1}\sqrt{n\epsilon^{2}\wedge(1\vee\rho^{-1/s})}}\bigwedge\left(\frac{\sqrt{1\vee\rho^{-1/s}}}{\sqrt{m}n\sqrt{n\epsilon^{2}\wedge 1}}\bigvee\frac{1}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right)\right)

and
M_{N}
satisfying (
S.91
). Solving the above display for
\rho
, we obtain the rate in Theorem
4
, (
10
).
∎

The proof is very similar to that of Theorem
4
in the sense that it follows from the lemmas proven earlier in the section.

In the case of local randomness, consider
T
as defined in (
32
), with
T_{\text{I}}
and
T_{\text{II}}
as defined in (
30
) and (
31
), respectively. Applying Lemma
22
with
{\mathcal{S}}=S^{\texttt{LOW}}
and Lemma
24
with
{\mathcal{S}}=S^{\texttt{HIGH}}
yields that
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T_{\text{I}}\leq\alpha/4
and
{\mathbb{P}}_{0}T_{\text{II}}\leq\alpha/4
, so
T
has the correct level.

If
f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{{s},R}_{p,q}
, with
s\in[s_{\min},s_{\max}]
, we have that
L_{s}
is either in
S^{\texttt{LOW}}
or
S^{\texttt{HIGH}}
. By applying Lemma
22
and Lemma
24
, a similar argument as given in the proof of Theorem
4
above yields that
{\mathbb{P}}_{f}(1-T)\leq\alpha/2
. The shared randomness case is analogous, with
T
defined as in (
34
).
∎

C
Addendum to the main results of Section
2

In this section, we provide additional discussion on the minimax testing rate under privacy constraints, presented in Section
2
.

We first consider the case of local randomness protocols, where the minimax rate is derived in Theorem
2
. The minimax separation rate satisfies (up to logarithmic factors) the relationship:

\rho^{2}\asymp\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{mn}\right)^{\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1/2}}+\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right)^{\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+3/2}}\wedge\left(\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\sqrt{m}n\sqrt{1\wedge n\epsilon^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1/2}}+\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right)\right).

We can find the dominant term in the above relationship for different values of
\epsilon
. For
s>1/4
, this yields that the minimax rate is given by

\displaystyle\rho^{2}\asymp\begin{cases}(mn/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2s}{2s+1/2}}&\text{ if }\epsilon\geq\sigma^{-\frac{2}{4s+1}}m^{\frac{1}{4s+1}}n^{\frac{1/2-2s}{4s+1}},\\
(mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2s}{2s+3/2}}&\text{ if }\sigma^{-\frac{2}{4s+1}}m^{\frac{1/4-s}{4s+1}}n^{\frac{1/2-2s}{4s+1}}\leq\epsilon<\sigma^{-\frac{2}{4s+1}}m^{\frac{1}{4s+1}}n^{\frac{1/2-2s}{4s+1}}\text{ and }\epsilon\geq n^{-1/2},\\
(mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2s}{2s+3/2}}&\text{ if }\sigma^{-\frac{4}{4s-1}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{\frac{5/2-2s}{4s-1}}\leq\epsilon<n^{-1/2},\\
(\sqrt{m}n/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2s}{2s+1/2}}&\text{ if }n^{-1/2}\leq\epsilon<\sigma^{-\frac{2}{4s+1}}m^{\frac{1/4-s}{4s+1}}n^{\frac{1/2-2s}{4s+1}},\\
(\sqrt{m}n^{3/2}\epsilon/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2s}{2s+1/2}}&\text{ if }\sigma^{\frac{1}{2s+1}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{-\frac{1+s}{2s+1}}\leq\epsilon<\sigma^{-\frac{4}{4s-1}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{\frac{5/2-2s}{4s-1}}\text{ and }\epsilon<n^{-1/2},\\
(mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2})^{-1}&\text{ if }\epsilon<\sigma^{\frac{1}{2s+1}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{-\frac{1+s}{2s+1}}.\end{cases}

(S.94)

Whenever
s\leq 1/4
, the first two cases in the above display become vacuous for
\epsilon\lesssim 1
, since
mn\to\infty
. Furthermore, the condition

\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right)^{\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+3/2}}\asymp\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\sqrt{m}n^{3/2}\epsilon}\right)^{\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1/2}}\iff\epsilon^{\frac{2s-1/2}{2s+3/2}}\asymp\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2s+3/2}}m^{\frac{1}{2s+3/2}}n^{\frac{5/4-s}{2s+3/2}},

cannot be satisfied when
s\leq 1/4
,
\epsilon\gtrsim(mn)^{-1}
and
\sigma^{2}=O(1)
. Consequently, whenever
s\leq 1/4
, the minimax rate simplifies to

\displaystyle\rho^{2}\asymp\begin{cases}(\sqrt{m}n/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2s}{2s+1/2}}&\text{ if }\epsilon\geq n^{-1/2},\\
(\sqrt{m}n^{3/2}\epsilon/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2s}{2s+1/2}}&\text{ if }\sigma^{\frac{2}{4s+1}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{-\frac{1+s}{2s+1}}\leq\epsilon<n^{-1/2},\\
(mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2})^{-1}&\text{ if }\epsilon<\sigma^{\frac{2}{4s+1}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{-\frac{1+s}{2s+1}}.\end{cases}

(S.95)

That is, the minimax rates corresponding to the high privacy-budget regimes cannot be attained for any value of
\epsilon
when
s\leq 1/4
.

Next, we consider the case of shared randomness protocols, where the minimax rate is derived in Theorem
1
. The minimax separation rate satisfies (up to logarithmic factors) the relationship:

\rho^{2}\asymp\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{mn}\right)^{\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1/2}}+\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{mn^{3/2}\epsilon\sqrt{1\wedge n\epsilon^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1}}\wedge\left(\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{\sqrt{m}n\sqrt{1\wedge n\epsilon^{2}}}\right)^{\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1/2}}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}}\right).

Finding the dominant term in the above relationship, we obtain that the minimax rate for shared randomness protocols is given by

\displaystyle\rho^{2}\asymp\begin{cases}(mn/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1/2}}&\text{ if }\epsilon\geq\sigma^{-\frac{2}{4s+1}}m^{\frac{1}{4{s}+1}}n^{\frac{1/2-2{s}}{4{s}+1}},\\
(mn^{3/2}\epsilon/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1}}&\text{ if }\sigma^{-\frac{2}{4s+1}}m^{-\frac{2{s}}{4{s}+1}}n^{\frac{1/2-2{s}}{4{s}+1}}\leq\epsilon<\sigma^{-\frac{2}{4s+1}}m^{\frac{1}{4{s}+1}}n^{\frac{1/2-2{s}}{4{s}+1}},\text{ and }\epsilon\geq n^{-1/2},\\
(mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1}}&\text{ if }\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2s}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{\frac{1-2{s}}{4{s}}}\leq\epsilon<n^{-1/2},\\
(\sqrt{m}n/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1/2}}&\text{ if }n^{-1/2}\leq\epsilon<\sigma^{-\frac{2}{4s+1}}m^{-\frac{2{s}}{4{s}+1}}n^{\frac{1/2-2{s}}{4{s}+1}},\\
(\sqrt{m}n^{3/2}\epsilon/\sigma^{2})^{-\frac{2{s}}{2{s}+1/2}}&\text{ if }\sigma^{\frac{1}{2s+1}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{-\frac{1+s}{2{s}+1}}\leq\epsilon<\sigma^{-\frac{1}{2s}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{\frac{1-2{s}}{4{s}}}\text{ and }\epsilon<n^{-1/2},\\
(mn^{2}\epsilon^{2}/\sigma^{2})^{-1}&\text{ if }\epsilon<\sigma^{\frac{1}{2s+1}}m^{-\frac{1}{2}}n^{-\frac{1+s}{2{s}+1}}.\end{cases}

(S.96)

Interestingly, even when
s\leq 1/4
, the high privacy-budget regimes occur for shared randomness protocols for some large enough value of
\epsilon
. This is in contrast to the case of local randomness protocols, where the minimax rates corresponding to the high privacy-budget regimes cannot be attained for any value of
\epsilon
when
s\leq 1/4
.

In Figure
below, we illustrate how the cost of privacy effectively increases the more data is distributed across multiple servers. In particular, we consider the case of
N=16
observations,
m=8
servers with each
n=2
observations. Even though the total number of observations is the same, comparison with Figure
2
shows that the separation rate in the more distributed setting (
m=8
) is much larger for any value of
\epsilon
.

D
Auxiliary lemmas

D.1
General privacy related lemmas

A random variable
V
is called
\nu
-
sub-gaussian
if

{\mathbb{P}}(|V|\geq t)\leq 2e^{-t^{2}/\nu^{2}},\;\text{ for all }t>0.

(S.97)

It is well known (see e.g.
[
83
]
) that if
{\mathbb{E}}V=0
, the above inequality holds if and only if

{\mathbb{E}}e^{tV}\leq e^{C\nu^{2}t^{2}},\;\text{ for all }t\geq 0,

for a constant
C>0
. A random variable
V
is called
\nu
-
sub-exponential
if

{\mathbb{P}}(|V|\geq t)\leq 2e^{-t/\nu},\;\text{ for all }t\geq 0.

(S.98)

If
{\mathbb{E}}V=0
, the above inequality holds if and only if
{\mathbb{E}}e^{tV}\leq e^{C\nu^{2}t^{2}}
for all
0\leq t\leq 1/(c\nu)
, with constants
c,C>0
.

The following lemma shows that clipping symmetric, mean zero random variables preserves sub-gaussianity and sub-exponentiality.

Lemma 27
.

Let
V_{1},\dots,V_{m}
denote independent random variables, each symmetrically distributed around zero. If
\sum_{j=1}^{m}V_{j}
are sub-gaussian (resp. sub-exponential) with parameter
\nu
, then so are the random variables
\sum_{j=1}^{m}[V_{j}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}
, for any
\tau>0
.

For any symmetric about
0
function
g:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
x\mapsto g(x)
is increasing on
[0,\infty)
, it holds that

g\left([x]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\right)\leq g\left(x\right),\quad\text{ for all }\tau>0.

(S.99)

Since
V_{j}
is symmetric about zero, so is
[V_{j}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}
. For an independent Rademacher random variable
R_{j}
, we have by the afformentioned symmetry about zero that
[V_{j}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\overset{d}{=}R_{j}[V_{j}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}
and consequently

{\mathbb{E}}e^{t[V_{j}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}}={\mathbb{E}}e^{tR_{j}[V_{j}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}}={\mathbb{E}}\cosh\left(t[V_{j}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\right).

Using the fact that
V_{1},\dots,V_{m}
are independent, we obtain that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}e^{t\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}V_{j}}

\displaystyle=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\prod}}{\mathbb{E}}\cosh\left(t[V_{j}]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\right)\leq\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\prod}}{\mathbb{E}}\cosh\left(tV_{j}\right),

where the inequality follows from (
S.99
). The conclusion can now be drawn from the moment generating function characterization of sub-gaussianity (resp. sub-exponentiality).
∎

The next lemma gives a lower bound on the expectation of a clipped random variable that is symmetric around a real number
\mu>0
.

Lemma 28
.

Let
\tau,\mu>0
satisfy
\tau/4\leq\mu\leq\tau/2
, let
V
be a random variable symmetric about zero (
V\overset{d}{=}-V
) with Lebesgue density bounded by
M>0
and

\text{Pr}\left(|V|\leq\frac{1}{12M}\vee(\tau/2)\right)\geq c

for some constant
c>0
. It then holds that

{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mu+V\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\geq(c\wedge 1/2)\mu.

(S.100)

By definition of clipping,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mu+V\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left[V\right]_{-\tau-\mu}^{\tau-\mu}+\mu.

The first term equals

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[V\right]_{-(\tau-\mu)}^{\tau-\mu}+{\mathbb{E}}\text{1}_{\{V\in[-\tau-\mu,{-\tau+\mu}]\}}\left(\left[V\right]_{-\tau-\mu}^{-\tau+\mu}+(\tau-\mu)\right)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[V\right]_{-(\tau-\mu)}^{\tau-\mu}-(\tau+\mu)\text{Pr}\left(-\tau-\mu\leq V\leq-\tau+\mu\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\tau+\mu)\text{Pr}\left(-\tau-\mu\leq V\leq-\tau+\mu\right),

where the last equality follows from the symmetry of
V
. By the condition on the Lebesgue density of
V
, the second term in the above display can be further bounded from below by
-2M(\tau+\mu)\mu
. When
3M\tau<1/2
, we obtain (
S.100
) with the constant
1/2
. Assume
3M\tau\geq 1/2
. Then, since
\mu>0
and
V
is symmetric about zero,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\left[\mu+V\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}\left(\mu+V\right)\text{1}\{|\mu+V|\leq\tau\}+\tau\left(\text{Pr}\left(\mu+V>\tau\right)-\text{Pr}\left(\mu+V<\tau\right)\right)

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{E}}\left(\mu+V\right)\text{1}\{|V|\leq\tau-\mu\}

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbb{E}}\left(\mu+V\right)\text{1}\{|V|\leq\tau/2\}

\displaystyle=\mu\text{Pr}\left(|V|\leq(1/12M)\vee(\tau/2)\right)

where the last inequality follows from
\mu\leq\tau/2
and the last equality follows from the symmetry of
V
about zero.
∎

Lemma 29
.

For any
\tau>0
and random variable
V
with
|{\mathbb{E}}V|\leq\tau
,

\text{Var}\left(\left[V\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\right)\leq\text{Var}\left(V\right).

If both
V
and
\left[V\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}
are mean
0
,
{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left[V\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\right)^{4}\leq{\mathbb{E}}V^{4}
.

Let
\mu={\mathbb{E}}V
. Since the expectation of a random variable is the constant minimizing the
L_{2}
-distance to that random variable,

\text{Var}\left(\left[V\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}\right)\leq{\mathbb{E}}\left(\left[V\right]_{-\tau}^{\tau}-\mu\right)^{2}.

The latter expectation can be written as

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\text{1}_{V\in[-\tau,\tau]}\left(V-\mu\right)^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}\text{1}_{V>\tau}\left(\tau-\mu\right)^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}\text{1}_{V<-\tau}\left(-\tau-\mu\right)^{2}.

Assuming
\mu\geq 0
,
V<-\tau
implies that
|-\tau-\mu|\leq|V-\mu|
. Since
\mu\leq\tau
,
V>\tau
implies that
|\tau-\mu|\leq|V-\mu|
. Consequently, the above display is bounded from above by

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}\text{1}_{V\in[-\tau,\tau]}\left(V-\mu\right)^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}\text{1}_{V>\tau}\left(V-\mu\right)^{2}+{\mathbb{E}}\text{1}_{V<-\tau}\left(V-\mu\right)^{2}={\mathbb{E}}\left(V-\mu\right)^{2}.

The case where
\mu<0
follows by the same reasoning. The last statement follows by a similar argument.
∎

D.2
General auxiliary lemmas

The following lemmas are well known but included for completeness.

Lemma 30
.

Consider for
L\in{\mathbb{N}}
and a nonnegative positive integer sequence
K_{n}
,

S_{n}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{K_{n}}}\underset{i=1}{\overset{K_{n}}{\sum}}\zeta_{i}

where
(\zeta_{1},\dots,\zeta_{K_{n}})
independent random variables with mean zero and unit variance.

Assume that the random variables satisfy Cramér’s condition, i.e. for some
\epsilon>0
and all
t\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
,
i=1,\dots,K_{n}
,

{\mathbb{E}}e^{t\zeta_{i}}<\infty.

Then, for any sequence of positive numbers
t_{n}
such that
K_{n}\gtrsim t_{n}^{3}
, it holds that

{\text{Pr}\left(S_{n}\geq t_{n}\right)}\leq O(1)\cdot e^{-t_{n}^{2}/2}.

By Cramér’s theorem (see e.g. Theorem 7 in Section 8.2 of
[
69
]
),

\frac{\text{Pr}\left(S_{n}\geq t_{n}\right)}{1-\Phi(t_{n})}=\exp\left(O(1)\cdot\frac{t_{n}^{3}}{\sqrt{K_{n}}}\right)\left(1+O\left(\frac{t_{n}}{\sqrt{K_{n}}}\right)\right)=O(1).

Note that the above statement holds for
-S_{n}
also. The statement now follows by using
1-\Phi(x)\leq e^{-x^{2}/2}
.
∎

The following lemma is a standard tail bound for the maximum of sub-gaussian (resp. sub-exponential) random variables that is used to control the type I error of the adaptive tests.

Lemma 31
.

Consider a sequence of subsets
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}\subset{\mathbb{N}}
and let
S_{n}(L)
be
\nu
-sub-gaussian. Then,

\text{Pr}\left(\underset{L\in{\mathcal{S}}_{n}}{\max}\;|S_{n}(L)|\geq t_{n}\right)\leq|{\mathcal{S}}_{n}|e^{-t_{n}^{2}/(2\nu^{2})}.

If
S_{n}(L)
is
\nu
-sub-exponential with sub-exponentiality constant equal to one, the statement is true for any positive sequence
C_{n}
and integer sequence
K_{n}
.

The proof follows immediately from union bounds and the properties of sub-gaussian (resp. sub-exponential random variables), see e.g. (
S.97
) (resp. (
S.98
)).

Pr

\displaystyle\left(\underset{L\in{\mathcal{S}}_{n}}{\max}\,S_{n}(L)\geq t_{n}\right)\leq\underset{L\in{\mathcal{S}}_{n}}{\overset{}{\sum}}\text{Pr}\left(|S_{n}(L)|\geq t_{n}\right)\leq 2|{\mathcal{S}}_{n}|e^{-t_{n}^{2}/(2\nu^{2})}.

The proof follows by showing that
S_{n}(L)
and
-S_{n}(L)
are or tend to sub-Gaussian variables with sub-gaussianity constant less than or equal to one, since this allows for bounding the above display by

\displaystyle 2\underset{L\in{\mathcal{S}}_{n}}{\overset{}{\sum}}e^{-\frac{c^{2}}{2}\log C_{n}}\leq\frac{2|{\mathcal{S}}_{n}|}{C_{n}^{c^{2}/2}}

and the result follows.

∎

Consider the following formal definition of coupling.

Definition 2
.

Consider probability measures
P
and
Q
on a measurable space
({\mathcal{X}},\mathscr{X})
. A
coupling
of
P
and
Q
is any probability measure
{\mathbb{P}}
on
({\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}},\mathscr{X}\otimes\mathscr{X})
such that
{\mathbb{P}}
has marginals
P
and
Q
:

P={\mathbb{P}}\circ\pi^{-1}_{1},\;Q={\mathbb{P}}\circ\pi^{-1}_{2}

where
\pi_{i}:{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{X}}\to{\mathcal{X}}
is the projection onto the
i
-th coordinate (i.e.
\pi_{i}(x_{1},x_{2})=x_{i}
for
i=1,2
).

Lemma
32
below is a well known result showing that, for random variables
X
and
\tilde{X}
defined on a Polish space, small total variation distance between their corresponding laws guarantees the existence of a coupling such that they are equal with high probability.

Lemma 32
.

For any two probability measures
P
and
Q
on a measurable space
({\mathcal{X}},\mathscr{X})
with
{\mathcal{X}}
a Polish space and
\mathscr{X}
its Borel sigma-algebra. There exists a coupling
{\mathbb{P}}^{X,\tilde{X}}
such that

\|P-Q\|_{TV}=2{\mathbb{P}}^{X,\tilde{X}}\left(X\neq\tilde{X}\right).

For a proof, see e.g. Section 8.3 in
[
79
]
.

The following lemma is well known and included for completeness.

Lemma 33
.

Let
P_{f}
denote the distribution of a
N(f,\sigma I_{d})
distributed random vector for
f\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
and let
P_{f}^{n}
denote the distribution of
n
i.i.d. draws (i.e.
P_{f}^{n}=\bigotimes_{i=1}^{n}P_{f}
).

It holds that

\left\|P_{f}^{n}-P_{g}^{n}\right\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\frac{n}{2\sigma}\left\|f-g\right\|_{2}.

By Pinsker’s inequality,

\|P_{f}^{n}-P_{g}^{n}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{n}{2}D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P_{f};{P}_{g})}.

A straightforward calculation gives that the latter is bounded by
\frac{\sqrt{n}}{2\sigma}\left\|f-g\right\|_{2}
.
∎

The following lemma bounds the maximum of a possibly correlated Gaussian random vector.

Lemma 34
.

Let
K\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
M\in{\mathbb{R}}^{K\times K}
be symmetric and positive definite. Consider the random vector
G=(G_{1},\dots,G_{K})\sim N(0,M)
. It holds that
{\mathbb{E}}\underset{1\leq i\leq K}{\max}|G_{i}|\leq 3\|M\|\sqrt{\log(K)\vee\log(2)}
and

\text{Pr}\left(\underset{1\leq i\leq K}{\max}G_{i}^{2}\geq\|M\|^{2}x\right)\leq\frac{2K}{e^{x/4}},

for all
x>0
.

It holds that

G\overset{d}{=}\sqrt{M}Z,\quad\text{ with }\quad Z\sim N(0,I_{K}).

Since
M
is symmetric, positive definite, it has SVD
M=V\text{Diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{K})V^{\top}
. Since
V
is orthonormal,

\sqrt{M}Z=V\sqrt{\text{Diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{K})}(V^{\top}Z)\overset{d}{=}V\sqrt{\text{Diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{K})}Z.

Writing
V=[v_{1}\;\dots\;v_{K}]
where
v_{k}
are orthogonal unit vectors, the latter display equals

\underset{k=1}{\overset{K}{\sum}}\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}v_{k}Z_{k}\;\sim\;N\left(0,\text{Diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{K})\right).

Consequently,

\underset{k\in[K]}{\max}|G_{k}|\overset{d}{=}\underset{k\in[K]}{\max}|\lambda_{k}Z_{k}|\leq\|M\|\underset{k\in[K]}{\max}|Z_{k}|.

Hence, it suffices to show that

\text{Pr}\left(\underset{1\leq i\leq K}{\max}Z_{i}^{2}\geq x\right)\leq\frac{2K}{e^{x/4}}

The case where
K=1
follows by standard Gaussian concentration properties. Assume
K\geq 2
. For
0\leq t\leq 1/4
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}e^{t\max_{i}(Z_{i})^{2}}=e^{t}{\mathbb{E}}\max_{i}e^{t(Z_{i}^{2}-1)}\leq Ke^{{2t^{2}}+t}.

Taking
t=1/4
and applying Markov’s inequality yields the second statement of the lemma. Furthermore, in view of Jensen’s inequality

{\mathbb{E}}\max_{i}(Z_{i})^{2}\leq\frac{\log(K)}{t}+2t+1,

which in turn yields
\max_{i}|Z_{i}|\leq 3\sqrt{\log(K)}
.
∎

The next lemma is a standard bound for the KL-divergence, see for instance Lemma 2.7 of
[
81
]
.

Lemma 35
.

Let
P,Q
probability measures on some measure space such that
Q\ll P
. Then,

D_{KL}(P\|Q)\leq\int\left(\frac{dP}{dQ}-1\right)^{2}dQ.

The following lemma is a standard concentration result for chi-square random variables, and is included for completeness.

Lemma 36
.

Let
X_{d}
be chi-square random variable with
d
-degrees of freedom. For
0<c<1
it holds that

\text{Pr}\left(X_{d}\leq cd\right)\leq e^{-d\frac{c-1-\log(c)}{2}}.

Similarly, for
c>1
it holds that

\text{Pr}\left(X_{d}\geq cd\right)\leq e^{-d\frac{c-1-\log(c)}{2}}.

Let
t<0
. We have

\displaystyle\text{Pr}\left(X_{d}\leq cd\right)

\displaystyle=\text{Pr}\left(e^{tX_{d}}\geq e^{tcd}\right)\leq\frac{{\mathbb{E}}e^{tX_{d}}}{e^{tcd}}.

Using that
{\mathbb{E}}e^{tX_{d}}=(1-2t)^{-d/2}
, the latter display equals

\exp\Big(-d\big(tc+\frac{1}{2}\log(1-2t)\big)\Big).

The expression
tc+\frac{1}{2}\log(1-2t)
is maximized when
t=\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{1}{c})<0
which leads to the result. The second statement follows by similar steps.
∎

Lemma 37
.

Let
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
and let
Z,Z^{\prime}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}N(0,I_{d})
for
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

Then,
a\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle
is
Ca^{2}
-sub-exponential for a universal constant
C>0
and

{\mathbb{E}}e^{t|a\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle|}\leq 2e^{{t^{2}a^{2}}},

whenever
|t|\leq(2a^{2})^{-1}
.

Since
\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle|Z^{\prime}\sim N(0,\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2})
,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}e^{ta\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{Z^{\prime}}{\mathbb{E}}^{Z|Z^{\prime}}e^{ta\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle}={\mathbb{E}}^{Z^{\prime}}e^{\frac{t^{2}a^{2}}{2}\|Z^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}}.

By standard arguments, the latter is further bounded by

e^{\frac{t^{2}a^{2}}{2}+\frac{t^{4}a^{4}}{2}}\leq e^{{t^{2}a^{2}}},

whenever
t^{2}a^{2}\leq 1/2
. The conclusion then follows by e.g. Proposition 2.7.1 in
[
83
]
, since
\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle
is mean zero. For the last statement,

\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle|Z^{\prime}\overset{d}{=}-\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle|Z^{\prime}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}^{Z|Z^{\prime}}e^{t|a\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle|}

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}^{Z|Z^{\prime}}\text{1}_{\{\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle>0\}}e^{ta\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle}+{\mathbb{E}}^{Z|Z^{\prime}}\text{1}_{\{\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle\leq 0\}}e^{-ta\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle}

\displaystyle\leq 2{\mathbb{E}}^{Z|Z^{\prime}}e^{ta\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle},

and the proof follows by what was shown above.
∎

Lemma 38
.

Let
S\sim\text{Bin}(p,n)
and
0\leq\epsilon\leq 1
. It holds that

{\mathbb{E}}Se^{\epsilon S}\leq e^{4\epsilon np}.

Write
S=\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}
, with
B_{1},\dots,B_{n}\overset{\text{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\text{Ber}(p)
.

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}Se^{\epsilon S}

\displaystyle=\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}{\mathbb{E}}B_{i}e^{\epsilon S}

\displaystyle=\underset{i=1}{\overset{n}{\sum}}pe^{\epsilon}{\mathbb{E}}e^{\epsilon\sum_{k\neq i}B_{k}}

\displaystyle=npe^{\epsilon}\left({\mathbb{E}}e^{\epsilon B_{1}}\right)^{n-1}

\displaystyle=npe^{\epsilon}\left(1+p(e^{\epsilon}-1)\right)^{n-1}

\displaystyle\leq e^{4np\epsilon},

where the inequality follows from the fact that
e^{x}-1\leq 2x
for
0\leq x\leq 1
.
∎

Lemma 39
.

Consider a sample space
{\mathcal{X}}
and a distributed protocol with Markov kernels
K^{j}:\mathscr{Y}^{(j)}\times{\mathcal{X}}\times{\mathcal{U}}\to[0,1]
for
j=1,\dots,m
and shared randomness distribution
{\mathbb{P}}^{U}
. Writing
\mathscr{Y}=\bigotimes^{m}_{j=1}\mathscr{Y}^{(j)}
for the product sigma-algebra, consider
K=\bigotimes^{m}_{j=1}K^{j}:\mathscr{Y}\times{\mathcal{X}}^{m}\times{\mathcal{U}}^{m}\to[0,1]
. It holds that

K(\cdot|x,u)\ll{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|U=u}_{f}(\cdot),\;\;{\mathbb{P}}^{(X,U)}_{f}-\text{almost surely,}

for all
f\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.

Let
A\in\mathscr{Y}^{(j)}
. We have that

B:=\left\{(x,u)\,:\,K^{j}(A|x,u)>0\right\}=\underset{l\in{\mathbb{N}}}{\bigcup}\left\{(x,u)\,:\,K^{j}(A|x,u)>\frac{1}{l}\right\},

so if
{\mathbb{P}}^{(X,U)}_{f}(B)>0
for some
L\in{\mathbb{N}}
it holds that
{\mathbb{P}}^{(X,U)}_{f}\left((x,u)\,:\,K^{j}(A|x,u)>\frac{1}{L}\right)>0
. Since
K^{j}
is nonnegative, by Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle{\mathbb{P}}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}_{f}(A)

\displaystyle=\int K^{j}(A|x,u)d{\mathbb{P}}^{X^{(j)}}_{f}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(x,u)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{B}K^{j}(A|x,u)d{\mathbb{P}}^{X^{(j)}}_{f}\times{\mathbb{P}}^{U}(x,u)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{L}{\mathbb{P}}^{(X,U)}_{f}\left((x,u)\,:\,K^{j}(A|x,u)>\frac{1}{L}\right)>0.

Since given
U
,
Y^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(m)}
, the statement for
K
follows as
K(\cdot|x^{1},\dots,x^{m},u):=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}K^{j}(\cdot|x^{j},u)
and
{\mathbb{P}}^{Y|U=u}_{f}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}{\mathbb{P}}^{Y^{(j)}|U=u}_{f}
.
∎

Lemma 40
.

Let
P=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}P_{j}
and
Q=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}Q_{j}
for probability measures
P_{j},Q_{j}
defined on a common measurable space
({\mathcal{X}},\mathscr{X})
, with probability densities
p_{j},q_{j}
for
j=1,\dots m
. It holds that

\|P-Q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}\leq\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}\|P_{j}-Q_{j}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

The measures
P_{j}
and
Q_{j}
admit densities with respect to
P_{j}+Q_{j}
, which we shall denote by
p_{j}
and
q_{j}
, respectively, with

\displaystyle p:=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}p_{j}=\frac{d\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}P_{j}}{d\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}(P_{j}+Q_{j})}\;\text{ and }\;q:=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}q_{j}=\frac{d\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}Q_{j}}{d\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}(P_{j}+Q_{j})}.

Writing
\mu=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}(P_{j}+Q_{j})
, we obtain

\|P-Q\|_{\mathrm{TV}}=\frac{1}{2}\int|\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}p_{j}(x_{j})-\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}q_{j}(x_{j})|d\mu(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}).

(S.101)

By the telescoping product identity

a_{1}\cdot a_{2}\cdots a_{m}-b_{1}\cdot b_{2}\cdots b_{m}=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}(a_{j}-b_{j})\underset{k=1}{\overset{j-1}{\Pi}}a_{k}\underset{k=j+1}{\overset{m}{\Pi}}b_{k}

(S.102)

and Fubini’s Theorem, the right-hand side of (
S.101
) is bounded by

\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}\frac{1}{2}\int|p_{j}(x_{j})-q_{j}(x_{j})|d(P_{j}+Q_{j})(x_{j})=\underset{j=1}{\overset{m}{\sum}}\|P_{j}-Q_{j}\|_{\mathrm{TV}}.

∎

E
Appendix on Besov norms in sequence space

In this section we briefly introduce Besov spaces as subspaces of
\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})
and collect some properties used in the paper.

For
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
1\leq p\leq\infty
and
1\leq q\leq\infty
, we define the Besov norm for
f\in\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})
as

\|f\|_{{\mathcal{B}}^{s}_{p,q}}:=\begin{cases}\left(\underset{l=1}{\overset{\infty}{\sum}}\left(2^{l({s}+1/2-1/p)}\left\|(f_{lk})_{k=1}^{2^{l}}\right\|_{p}\right)^{q}\right)^{1/q}&\text{ for }1\leq q<\infty,\\
\;\underset{l\geq 1}{\sup}\;2^{l({s}+1/2-1/p)}\left\|(f_{lk})_{k=1}^{2^{l}}\right\|_{p}&\text{ for }q=\infty.\end{cases}

This definition of the Besov norm is equivalent to the Besov norm as typically defined on a function space when considering the wavelet transform of a function in
L_{2}[0,1]
,

\displaystyle f=\sum_{j=j_{0}}^{\infty}\sum_{k=0}^{2^{j}-1}f_{jk}\psi_{jk},

where

\displaystyle\big\{\phi_{j_{0}m},\psi_{jk}:\,m\in\{0,\ldots,2^{j_{0}}-1\},\quad j>j_{0},\quad k\in\{0,\ldots,2^{j}-1\}\big\},

are the orthogonal wavelet basis functions, for father
\phi(.)
and mother
\psi(.)
wavelets with
N
vanishing moments and bounded support on
[0,2N-1]
and
[-N+1,N]
, respectively, following e.g. the construction of Cohen, Daubechies and Vial
[
29
,
31
]
, with
N>s
. The Besov norm on the function space is then equivalent to the one defined above for the wavelet coefficients
(f_{jk})_{j\geq j_{0},k\in\{0,...,2^{j}-1\}}
. See e.g. Chapter 4 in
[
43
]
for details.

Let
{\mathcal{B}}^{s}_{p,q}(R)\subset\ell_{2}({\mathbb{N}})
denote the Besov ball of radius
R
. The following lemmas are standard results, for which we provide a proof for completeness.

Lemma 41
.

There exists a constant
C_{s,q}>0
such that
\|f\|_{2}\leq C_{s,q}R
for all
f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{s}_{p,q}(R)
with
1\leq q\leq\infty
and
2\leq p\leq\infty
.

Using that
p\geq 2
, Hölder’s inequality yields that

\displaystyle\|(f_{lk})_{k=1}^{2^{l}}\|_{2}\leq 2^{1/2-1/p}\|(f_{lk})_{k=1}^{2^{l}}\|_{p}.

Define
c_{s}:=1/(1-2^{-s})
. By another application of Hölder’s inequality, we obtain that

\underset{l=1}{\overset{\infty}{\sum}}2^{1/2-1/p}\|(f_{lk})_{k=1}^{2^{l}}\|_{p}\leq c_{s}^{1-1/q}\left\|\left(2^{l(s+1/2-1/p)}\|(f_{lk})_{k=1}^{2^{l}}\|_{p}\right)_{l=1}^{\infty}\right\|_{q}.

∎

Using a similar proof, one obtains also the following lemma, see e.g. Chapter 9 in
[
48
]
.

Lemma 42
.

Let
f_{lk}
are the wavelet coefficients of the function
f\in B^{\alpha}_{p,q}(R)
. For any
1\leq q\leq\infty
,
2\leq p\leq\infty
,
L>0
, we have

\sum_{l>L}\sum_{k=0}^{2^{l}-1}f_{lk}^{2}\leq c_{\alpha}2^{-2L\alpha}R^{2},

where
c_{\alpha}>0
is a universal constant depending only on
\alpha
.

Lemma 43
.

There exists a constant
C_{\alpha,R}>0
such that
\|f\|_{\infty}\leq C_{\alpha,R}
for all
f\in B^{\alpha}_{p,q}(R)
with
\alpha-1/2-1/p>0
,
1\leq q\leq\infty
and
2\leq p\leq\infty
.

Lemma 44
.

Let
f_{lk}
are the wavelet coefficients of the function
f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{s}_{p,q}(R)
. For any
1\leq q\leq\infty
,
2\leq p\leq\infty
,
L>0
, we have

\sum_{l>L}\sum_{k=0}^{2^{l}-1}f_{lk}^{2}\leq C_{s,q}2^{-2Ls}R^{2},

where
C_{s,q}>0
is a universal constant depending only on
s
and
q
.
